name: caller-lead-finder
description: The complete blueprint of the Caller Lead Finder, the part of my Lead CRM app that finds local businesses losing phone calls so I can build them an AI receptionist and sell it. Read this skill before building or changing anything in that finder, and build only when asked, one piece at a time.
---

# Caller Lead Finder, the blueprint

This lives inside the Lead CRM app. One search prints 40 to 50 real businesses nearby, keeps the ones with a phone number, works out when they shut, and saves them into the Leads list through save_lead.

Rules for using this skill:
- Never build the whole thing in one go. The student asks for one piece at a time. Build exactly the piece they name, nothing more.
- If a request conflicts with this blueprint, follow the blueprint and say so.
- The Google key lives in a Secret and is never shown on any page or in any reply.
- Nothing writes into the Leads list except save_lead. Never write to it directly.

## The pieces

### Piece 1: the search (backend function: search_callers)
- Inputs: business_type (like "plumber") and suburb (like "Springvale VIC").
- Calls Google Places API (New) Text Search using the GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY secret, server side only.
- Runs FOUR query variations: "[type] in [suburb]", "[type] services in [suburb]", "[type] company in [suburb]", "[type] business in [suburb]".
- Pulls up to 3 pages of results per variation.
- Combines everything and removes duplicates by google_place_id.
- For each business returns: name, phone, address, rating, review count, opening hours, google_place_id.
- THE FILTER THAT IS THIS BUSINESS: keep only results that have a phone number. A business with no phone cannot miss a call, so it is not a lead for this service.
- Typical result: 40 to 50 unique businesses per search before the filter.

### Piece 2: the closing-time read
- For every kept business, read the opening hours and work out the earliest weekday closing time.
- Save that as closes_at, in plain 24 hour text like "17:00". If Google gives no hours at all, save "unknown".
- Set gap from the hours:
  - "closes early" when the earliest weekday close is 17:00 or before
  - "closed weekends" when Saturday and Sunday are both shut
  - "unknown hours" when Google has no hours
  - "open late" when they trade past 17:00 every weekday
- Tag lead_quality "hot" when the business has 10 or more reviews AND a rating of 4.0 or higher AND gap is not "open late". Otherwise "normal".
- Nothing gets dropped for being "open late". A business that trades late still misses calls when everyone is on a job. The tag just says who to call first.

### Piece 3: the smart suburb box (backend function: autocomplete_suburb)
- As the student types a suburb, suggest real suburbs using Google Places Autocomplete.
- Start suggesting after 2 characters. Debounce so it does not fire on every keystroke.

### Piece 4: feeding the CRM
- Every kept business goes into the Leads list by calling save_lead, one at a time.
- service is always "caller". gap is the gap worked out in piece 2. source is "caller finder".
- Also pass closes_at through so it is stored on the lead.
- save_lead handles duplicates. The finder never checks for them itself and never writes to the list directly.
- After a run, report back: how many businesses Google returned, how many had a phone number, how many were new, how many already existed.

### Piece 5: the search screen
- One page inside the Lead CRM app. Two boxes: business type, and suburb (powered by autocomplete_suburb). A Search button.
- While it runs, show what it is doing. When it finishes, show the four numbers from piece 4 and a link through to the Leads screen.
- Plain and easy to read. No dashboard, no charts.

### Piece 6: the secret shop record
- Two extra fields on a lead: secret_shop_result, one of "no answer", "voicemail", "answered", or empty until it is tried, and secret_shop_date.
- On the Leads screen, a one-click control to set secret_shop_result, which stamps secret_shop_date with today.

## The secret
- GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY holds the student's own Google key. Backend functions read it. Nothing else ever does. It never appears in a page, a reply, or a log.

## Never do
- Never invent businesses, phone numbers or opening hours. Everything comes from the live Google results.
- Never write into the Leads list except through save_lead.
- Never drop a business for trading late without being asked. Late traders still miss calls.
- Never guess a closing time. If Google has no hours, gap is "unknown hours" and the student finds out by calling.

Once it's saved, play the blueprint back to me in one short paragraph so I know you've got the whole machine. Don't build anything yet.

---
name: lead-crm
description: The complete blueprint of the Lead CRM app, the one place every business I might contact lives, whichever service I sell. Read this skill before building or changing anything in that app, and build only when asked, one piece at a time.
---

# Lead CRM, the blueprint

This is the one list. Every business found by any finder ends up here, gets worked, and never gets lost or rung twice.

Rules for using this skill:
- Never build the whole app in one go. The student asks for one piece at a time. Build exactly the piece they name, nothing more.
- If a request conflicts with this blueprint, follow the blueprint and say so.
- The student's own notes and status are sacred. Nothing automatic ever overwrites them.

## The pieces

### Piece 1: the Leads list
Each row is one business. Fields:
- business_name, phone, email, website, address, suburb
- category, the kind of business it is
- google_place_id, Google's own ID for that business, unique
- rating, review_count
- service, which of my three services this lead is for: "website", "caller" or "walkthrough"
- gap, one short line on what they are missing
- status, one of: new, calling, called, interested, not interested, sold
- last_contacted, a date
- notes, a long text box
- source, where the lead came from
New rows always start at status "new".

### Piece 2: save_lead, the one door in
A backend function. Everything that adds a lead goes through this and nothing else.
It takes: business_name, phone, email, website, address, suburb, category, google_place_id, rating, review_count, service, gap, source.
How it behaves:
1. No row with that google_place_id yet, add a new one with status "new".
2. A row already exists, update it, but only the fields actually passed in. If a field is not passed, leave what is already there. Never wipe a field by passing nothing.
3. Never touch status, notes or last_contacted. Those belong to the student.
4. Report back whether it added a new one or updated an existing one.

### Piece 3: the screen
A table of every lead showing business name, phone, suburb, service, status and when they were last contacted.
At the top, a search box for the business name and buttons to filter by status and by service.
Status can be changed straight from the table without opening anything. Clicking a row opens it so notes can be read and written.
Sort so leads that have not been contacted come first.
It has to work on a phone, because the student reads it while they are on calls.

### Piece 4: the nightly job
An automation that runs every night, brings in new businesses through save_lead, and reports in the morning: how many new leads, and which suburb.

### Piece 5: enrichment
For leads that have not been enriched yet, look at what is public about them and add short notes: whether they have a website and whether it looks old, whether they are on social media and when they last posted, what their reviews say, and one specific thing worth mentioning on a call.
Six lines maximum per lead. Never invent anything. "Not found" is a fine answer.

## Never do
- Never invent a business, a phone number or a review. Everything comes from real results.
- Never add a lead by any route other than save_lead.
- Never overwrite the student's status or notes.
- Never let the same business appear twice.

Once it's saved, play the blueprint back to me in one short paragraph so I know you've got the whole machine. Don't build anything yet.

name: walkthrough-lead-finder
description: The complete blueprint of the Walkthrough Lead Finder, the part of my Lead CRM app that finds real estate agents with properties listed and no video, so I can sell them walkthrough videos. Read this skill before building or changing anything in that finder, and build only when asked, one piece at a time.
---

# Walkthrough Lead Finder, the blueprint

This lives inside the Lead CRM app. It is deliberately half automatic and half done by hand, and that is not a shortcut.

Why: property listing sites block apps from reading their listings, and their terms do not allow it. Anything that claims to scrape them stops working. So the app finds the agencies, and the student looks at the listings themselves.

Rules for using this skill:
- Never build the whole thing in one go. The student asks for one piece at a time. Build exactly the piece they name, nothing more.
- If a request conflicts with this blueprint, follow the blueprint and say so.
- Never suggest scraping a property site, and never build anything that reads one. If the student asks, explain why and offer this instead.
- Nothing writes into the Leads list except save_lead.

## The pieces

### Piece 1: the agency search (backend function: search_agencies)
- Input: suburb.
- Calls Google Places API (New) Text Search using the GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY secret, server side only, with the query "real estate agency in [suburb]".
- Pulls up to 3 pages. Returns name, phone, address, website, rating, review count and google_place_id.

### Piece 2: feeding the CRM
- Every agency found goes into the Leads list by calling save_lead. service is "walkthrough", source is "agency finder", gap is "not checked yet".
- Report how many were new and how many already existed.

### Piece 3: the listing fields
- Three extra fields on a lead: agent_name, listing_address, listing_url.
- save_lead accepts all three, following its usual rules: only update what is passed in, never wipe anything, never touch status or notes.
- Show all three on the lead panel.

### Piece 4: adding a listing from a paste
- The student pastes a property address, an agent name and an agency. It goes in through save_lead with service "walkthrough" and gap "no video on listing".
- If that agency is already a lead, add the agent name and listing to the existing lead rather than creating a second one.
- Confirm back what was added or updated.

### Piece 5: finding the agent
- For a walkthrough lead with an agent_name, look up that agent's own public profile on their agency site and find their direct phone or email.
- Save what is found through save_lead. If nothing is public, write "not found". Never guess a number.

### Piece 6: the weekly reminder
- Every Sunday evening, remind the student to spend 20 minutes checking new listings for missing video.
- Include how many walkthrough leads they have and how many they have contacted.

## Never do
- Never read, scrape or crawl a property listing site, whatever the student asks.
- Never invent an agent, a phone number or a property address.
- Never write into the Leads list except through save_lead.

Once it's saved, play the blueprint back to me in one short paragraph so I know you've got the whole machine. Don't build anything yet.

name: website-lead-finder
description: The complete blueprint of the Website Lead Finder, the part of my Lead CRM app that finds local businesses with no website so I can build them one and sell it. Read this skill before building or changing anything in that finder, and build only when asked, one piece at a time.
---

# Website Lead Finder, the blueprint

This lives inside the Lead CRM app. One search prints 40 to 50 real businesses nearby, throws away everyone who already has a website, and saves the rest into the Leads list through save_lead.

Rules for using this skill:
- Never build the whole thing in one go. The student asks for one piece at a time. Build exactly the piece they name, nothing more.
- If a request conflicts with this blueprint, follow the blueprint and say so.
- The Google key lives in a Secret and is never shown on any page or in any reply.
- Nothing writes into the Leads list except save_lead. Never write to it directly.

## The pieces

### Piece 1: the search (backend function: search_websites)
- Inputs: business_type (like "plumber") and suburb (like "Springvale VIC").
- Calls Google Places API (New) Text Search using the GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY secret, server side only.
- Runs FOUR query variations: "[type] in [suburb]", "[type] services in [suburb]", "[type] company in [suburb]", "[type] business in [suburb]".
- Pulls up to 3 pages of results per variation.
- Combines everything and removes duplicates by google_place_id.
- For each business returns: name, phone, address, rating, review count, opening hours, websiteUri, google_place_id.
- Typical result: 40 to 50 unique businesses per search before the filter.

### Piece 2: the filter that is this business
- Keep only results where websiteUri is missing or empty. Everyone with a website gets dropped.
- For each kept business set gap to "no website".
- Tag lead_quality "hot" when the business has 10 or more reviews AND a rating of 4.0 or higher. Otherwise "normal".

### Piece 3: the smart suburb box (backend function: autocomplete_suburb)
- As the student types a suburb, suggest real suburbs using Google Places Autocomplete.
- Start suggesting after 2 characters. Debounce so it does not fire on every keystroke.

### Piece 4: feeding the CRM
- Every kept business goes into the Leads list by calling save_lead, one at a time.
- service is always "website". gap is "no website". source is "website finder".
- save_lead handles duplicates. The finder never checks for them itself and never writes to the list directly.
- After a run, report back: how many businesses Google returned, how many had no website, how many were new, how many already existed.

### Piece 5: the search screen
- One page inside the Lead CRM app. Two boxes: business type, and suburb (powered by autocomplete_suburb). A Search button.
- While it runs, show what it is doing. When it finishes, show the four numbers from piece 4 and a link through to the Leads screen.
- Plain and easy to read. No dashboard, no charts.

## The secret
- GOOGLE_PLACES_API_KEY holds the student's own Google key. Backend functions read it. Nothing else ever does. It never appears in a page, a reply, or a log.

## Never do
- Never invent businesses or details. Everything comes from the live Google results.
- Never write into the Leads list except through save_lead.
- Never loosen the no-website filter without being asked. A business with a website is not a lead for this service.

Once it's saved, play the blueprint back to me in one short paragraph so I know you've got the whole machine. Don't build anything yet.

name: receptionist-agent-builder
description: Creates the Retell voice agent for an AI receptionist, using the proven v3 recipe. Short role prompt, business facts in a knowledge base, behaviour rules in tool descriptions, all logic on the server. Run this only after the booking backend exists and its URLs are live.
---

# Receptionist Agent Builder

## What this skill is for

You are building the voice. The agent that picks up the phone, talks like a person,
takes a booking and hands over to a human when it has to.

Do not run this until `receptionist-booking-backend` has finished and you have the
live function URLs in front of you. Tools attached to a URL that does not exist yet
fail on every call, and a published agent cannot simply be edited afterwards.

## Parameters you need before you start

- `{BUSINESS_NAME}` the client's business name, spelled the way they spell it.
- `{BUSINESS_TYPE}` for example plumbing, carpet cleaning, mobile dog grooming.
- `{WEBSITE_URLS}` the specific pages worth reading: home, services, pricing,
  contact, service area.
- `{BOOKING_API_URL}` the live `booking_api` URL from the backend skill.
- `{TRANSFER_NUMBER}` the mobile the agent passes callers to, in full
  international format. This must NOT be the business number. See the loop trap in
  `receptionist-client-intake`.
- `{VOICE_ID}` the Retell voice.
- `{LANGUAGE}` the accent code, for example `en-AU`.
- `{RETELL_API_KEY}` from the owner's secrets.
- `{NEVER_SAY}` the list of things the client does not want promised.

Ask for anything missing. Never guess a transfer number.

## The v3 recipe, which is the whole point of this skill

Four rules. They are not style preferences. Version 2 of this build ignored them,
invented callout fees on a real call, interrogated the caller with question after
question, and had to be thrown away.

1. **The agent talks.** The role prompt holds identity, tone and hard guardrails.
   A few tight paragraphs. Nothing else.
2. **The knowledge base knows.** Services, prices, hours, service area, the answers
   to the questions people ring up and ask. Facts live here, never in the prompt.
   Facts in the prompt make the model both inconsistent and chatty.
3. **The tool descriptions decide behaviour.** When to fire a tool and exactly what
   to pass. This is where the behaviour rules go, not the prompt.
4. **The server does the work.** Dates, validation, availability, saving. The model
   only converses and extracts.

Say it back to the owner in one line so they can hold it: the agent talks, the
knowledge base knows, the server decides.

## Step 1: create the LLM

Create a Retell LLM using model `gpt-4.1`.

Gotchas that will cost you an hour if you skip them:

- To read an LLM back, the call is `get-retell-llm`, not `get-llm`.
- Tools live on the **LLM** object, under `general_tools`. `get-agent` returns
  tools as `null` even when the agent has four working tools. That is normal. Do
  not conclude the agent has no tools and rebuild it.
- If the Retell MCP connection returns `Invalid API Key` while the same key works
  fine in a direct request, the key is fine and MCP is not. Fall back to calling
  the Retell REST API directly with curl and carry on.

## Step 2: the role prompt

Keep it this short. Fill in the braces, change the tone line to suit the business,
and change nothing else. Adapt per client, keep it this short.

```
You are the receptionist for {BUSINESS_NAME}, a {BUSINESS_TYPE} business.
You answer the phone when the team cannot get to it.

How you sound
Warm, quick and normal. Like a good receptionist who has worked here for years.
Short sentences. One question at a time. Never read a list at someone.
If the caller goes off topic, follow them for a moment, then bring it back.

What you do
Find out who is calling, what they need and when they want it, and book it in.
Answer questions about the business from what you know.
If someone clearly wants a human, pass them through.

Hard rules
Never invent a price, a date, a time, an availability or a policy. If you do not
know something, say you will have someone confirm it, and take their details.
Never promise anything that is not in what you know about this business.
Never say you are an AI unless you are asked directly. If you are asked, say yes
plainly and keep going.
{NEVER_SAY}
```

That is the whole prompt. If you are tempted to paste the price list into it, that
is the failure this recipe exists to prevent. It goes in the knowledge base.

## Step 3: the knowledge base

Create a Retell knowledge base from the client's real website and attach it to the
agent.

Rules, learned the hard way:

- The create-knowledge-base call with URLs requires **multipart form data**. A JSON
  body returns a 500 with no useful message. Send it as multipart.
- The parameter is `knowledge_base_urls`, plus `enable_auto_refresh`.
- The API takes **specific URLs**, one per page. Whole-site auto-crawl is a
  dashboard feature only. Give it the home page, the services page, the pricing
  page, the contact page and the service area page. Five good URLs beat a guess at
  the site root.
- **Resolve redirects first.** If a URL 301s, the crawl can come back empty. Follow
  each URL to where it actually lands and use the final address.
- After it builds, read three facts out of the knowledge base and check them
  against the website with your own eyes. If the prices in the knowledge base do
  not match the website, the agent will quote the wrong number to a real customer.

If the client has no website, ask the owner for the facts in writing and create the
knowledge base from a text document instead. Never fill the gap with guesses.

## Step 4: the tools

Attach four tools to the LLM.

### 1. end_call

Retell's native tool. Nothing to configure.

### 2. transfer_to_human

- Type: `transfer_call`
- Transfer type: **cold transfer**
- Destination: `{TRANSFER_NUMBER}`, predefined
- `speak_after_execution`: true
- Description: `Transfer the caller to a human only when the caller clearly asks
  for a person, or says it is an emergency. Tell them you are putting them through
  before you transfer.`

### 3. check_availability

- Type: custom
- Method: POST
- URL: `{BOOKING_API_URL}`
- Description, which is where the behaviour lives:

```
Use this when the caller says when they would like the job done. Pass the date
exactly as the caller said it. If they said today, pass the literal word today. If
they said tomorrow, pass the literal word tomorrow. If they said a weekday, pass
that weekday name. Never work out a calendar date yourself and never pass a date
you calculated. The system knows what today is and you do not. Read back the times
it gives you in natural speech, like "I've got two o'clock or half three", never as
a raw list.
```

### 4. create_booking

- Type: custom
- Method: POST
- URL: `{BOOKING_API_URL}`
- `payload_type`: `args_only`
- `speak_during_execution`: true, with a short filler line such as
  `Let me check what we've got available.`
- `speak_after_execution`: true
- Description:

```
Use this once you have the caller's name, phone number, address, what they need
and a time they have agreed to. Pass the date exactly as the caller said it,
including the literal words today or tomorrow. Never calculate a date. If the
system says that time has gone, offer the alternatives it returns.
```

Notice what is happening: every rule about behaviour is sitting in a tool
description, where the model reads it at the moment it matters, instead of being
buried in a prompt it read once at the start of the call.

## Step 5: voice and language

- Set language to `{LANGUAGE}`.
- Set the voice to `{VOICE_ID}`. `11labs-Noah` is proven on a real call with an
  Australian accent and sounds right for trades.
- Other Australian options to offer the owner: built-in `retell-Willa`,
  `retell-Nico`, `retell-Alejandro`, `retell-Leland`; ElevenLabs `11labs-Willa`,
  `11labs-Lily`, `11labs-Marissa`, `11labs-Dorothy`; Cartesia `cartesia-Cleo`,
  `cartesia-Summer`, `cartesia-Emily`, `cartesia-Evie`; OpenAI `openai-Nova`.
- Friendly and casual for trades and cleaning. Professional and calm for medical or
  legal. Play two to the owner before you pick.

## Step 6: post-call analysis

Set the post-call analysis model to `gpt-4.1` and add extraction fields the owner
will actually read:

- `caller_name`
- `caller_number`
- `what_they_wanted`
- `booking_made` (yes or no)
- `needs_follow_up` (yes or no)

## Step 7: webhooks

- Agent webhook, for call events: the `retell_webhook` URL from the backend skill.
- Inbound webhook, set later on the phone number, not here: the
  `inbound_call_router` URL. That belongs to `retell-number-connect`.

## Step 8: publish, and the rule that comes with it

Publish the agent.

**A published Retell agent and its LLM are locked.** You cannot edit them in place.
When something needs to change after publishing:

1. Clone the LLM.
2. Make the change on the clone.
3. Create a new agent version pointing at the clone.
4. Repoint the phone number at the new agent.

Do not fight the version lock, do not try to force an edit through, and do not
delete a working agent to make a change. Clone and repoint. That is the whole
answer.

## Read-back verification, before you say the agent is built

Print these and check each one against what the owner asked for:

1. The agent name, the agent id, and the LLM id.
2. The full role prompt. Confirm out loud that it contains no prices, no hours and
   no service list.
3. The knowledge base id, the exact URLs it crawled, and three facts read out of
   it that you have checked against the website.
4. All four tools, read off the **LLM** object. For each custom tool, read the URL
   character by character against `{BOOKING_API_URL}`.
5. The transfer number, digit by digit, and confirm with the owner that it is not
   the business number.
6. The voice id and the language.
7. Published: yes, and the version number.

Then hand over to `receptionist-test-protocol` and run test step 1, the dashboard
web call, before anything touches a real phone number.

---
name: receptionist-booking-backend
description: Builds the booking backend for an AI receptionist inside a Base44 app. Creates the Booking entity, the booking_api function that checks availability and creates bookings, the inbound_call_router function and the retell_webhook function. Run this BEFORE the Retell agent is created, so the tool URLs already exist when the tools are attached.
---

# Receptionist Booking Backend

## What this skill is for

An AI receptionist needs somewhere to put a booking before it can take one. This
skill builds that place. You build it first, every time, because the voice agent's
tools need real working URLs the moment they are attached. Building the agent first
and the backend second is the single most common way this build breaks.

You build four things inside the owner's Base44 app:

1. The **Booking** entity, the table every booking lands in.
2. The **booking_api** function, the one endpoint the voice agent calls.
3. The **inbound_call_router** function, which tells Retell which agent answers.
4. The **retell_webhook** function, which receives call events after each call.

## Parameters you need before you start

Ask the owner for these and never assume them. Everything in this skill that looks
like `{THIS}` is filled in from the answers.

- `{APP_NAME}` the Base44 app you are building inside.
- `{APP_URL}` the app's live base URL, for example `https://your-app.base44.app`.
  You read this off the app itself. Never reuse a URL from an example.
- `{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}` the IANA timezone of the client's business, for example
  `Australia/Brisbane`. This is the client's timezone, not yours, not the server's.
- `{OPEN_TIME}` and `{CLOSE_TIME}` the hours bookings can be made in.
- `{SLOT_MINUTES}` how long one booking slot is.
- `{SERVICE_LIST}` the services the business books.
- `{RETELL_API_KEY}` stored in the app's secrets, never pasted into a page or a
  prompt in plain text.

If you do not have one of these, ask one short question and wait. Do not invent a
default.

## Rule zero: the server decides

Write this down and keep it true through the whole build.

- The agent talks.
- The knowledge base knows.
- **The server decides.**

Every date, every validation, every "is that slot free", every write to the
database happens in `booking_api`, in code, on the server. The language model
never resolves a date, never checks a calendar, never decides whether a booking is
valid. If you find yourself putting that logic in a prompt, stop and put it here
instead.

## Part 1: the Booking entity

Create an entity called `Booking` with exactly these fields:

- `name` (text) the caller's full name
- `phone` (text) the caller's phone number
- `address` (text) where the job is
- `date` (text) the resolved booking date in `YYYY-MM-DD`
- `time` (text) the booking time in 24 hour `HH:MM`
- `service` (text) what they need done
- `status` (choice: pending, confirmed, completed, cancelled. Default: pending)

Optional fields, add them only if the owner asks:

- `notes` (text) anything else the caller said
- `urgency` (choice: routine, urgent, emergency)
- `call_id` (text) the Retell call this booking came from

Read the field list back to the owner before you move on.

## Part 2: the booking_api function

One backend function, two actions. The voice agent's two tools both point at it.

### Action: check_availability

Input: `date` (a string that may be the literal word `today`, the literal word
`tomorrow`, a weekday name, or a `YYYY-MM-DD` date).

What the server does, in this order:

1. Resolve the date. Take the current time in `{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}`. If the input is
   `today`, that is today's date in that timezone. If it is `tomorrow`, add one
   day. If it is a weekday name, take the next occurrence of that weekday. If it
   is already `YYYY-MM-DD`, use it as given.
2. Reject the past. If the resolved date is before today in `{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}`,
   return an error that says the date has already gone and asks for another day.
   Never write a past booking.
3. Build the day's slots from `{OPEN_TIME}` to `{CLOSE_TIME}` in `{SLOT_MINUTES}`
   steps.
4. Remove any slot that already has a Booking on that date with status `pending`
   or `confirmed`.
5. Return the resolved date and a short list of free times. Two or three options
   is plenty. A voice agent reading out fourteen times is unusable.

### Action: create_booking

Input: `name`, `phone`, `address`, `date`, `time`, `service`.

What the server does, in this order:

1. Resolve `date` exactly the way `check_availability` does. Same code path, not
   a copy.
2. Reject the past, same rule.
3. Check the slot is still free. If it is not, return the next two free times so
   the agent can offer them instead of failing.
4. Check for a duplicate first: same name, same date, same time already saved
   means the caller repeated themselves or the agent retried. Return the existing
   booking instead of writing a second row.
5. Create the Booking row with status `confirmed`. The agent confirms with the
   caller on the phone, so there is no pending step in V1.
6. Return a short confirmation with the resolved date and time in words the agent
   can say back, for example `Thursday the 11th at 2pm`.

### The message field is the steering wheel

Every response `booking_api` returns carries a `message` field, and that message
is not for the caller. It is the next instruction to the voice agent. `Booked:
Sarah tomorrow at 2pm. Read it back once, mention the callout fee, then wrap up`
tells the agent exactly what to do next. `No slots left on that day. Offer the
next day` steers it out of a dead end. Write every message as an instruction to
the agent, never as a raw data dump, and the conversation stays on rails even
when things go wrong.

### The date rule, stated once so nobody is confused

The agent is told, in its tool descriptions, to pass the caller's words: the
literal word `today`, the literal word `tomorrow`, or a weekday name. The server
turns that into a real date. This exists because a voice model has no idea what
today's date is and will happily guess a date two years in the past. Every date
lands on the server or it is wrong.

### Signature verification

Retell signs every custom tool request with HMAC-SHA256 and sends it in the
`X-Retell-Signature` header. `booking_api` verifies that signature against the
Retell API key held in the app's secrets, and rejects anything that fails. Do this
before you read the body. Anyone who finds the URL can otherwise write bookings
into the client's database. Implement the check per Retell's current signature
docs and prove it with a real call: one call that books successfully WITH
verification on is the test. `[VERIFY: confirm the exact signature format against
docs.retellai.com before a client build goes live.]`

### Reference implementation, shaped by a live build

The code below is the working shape from a receptionist that took real bookings
on real phone calls. Use it as the skeleton and fill every `{PLACEHOLDER}` from
the owner's answers. The parts that earn their keep: the date resolver that
accepts the literal words the agent passes, the time normaliser that survives
"9am" and "midday", the duplicate check, the spoken-friendly times, and error
responses that always return HTTP 200 with an instruction in `message` (a non-200
makes the voice agent stall mid-call).

```typescript
import { createClientFromRequest } from 'npm:@base44/sdk@0.8.31';

// booking_api - the one endpoint the voice agent calls.
Deno.serve(async (req) => {
  const headers = { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' };
  try {
    let body: any = {};
    try { body = await req.json(); } catch { /* empty body */ }
    console.log('booking_api name:', body.name, 'args:', JSON.stringify(body.args));

    const toolName = body.name || body.tool_name || '';
    const args = body.args || body.parameters || {};

    const TIMEZONE = '{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}';            // e.g. 'Australia/Brisbane'
    // Every bookable time from {OPEN_TIME} to {CLOSE_TIME} in {SLOT_MINUTES} steps.
    const SLOTS = ['07:00','08:00','09:00','10:00','11:00','12:00',
                   '13:00','14:00','15:00','16:00','17:00'];
    const DEFAULT_SERVICE = '{most common service from SERVICE_LIST}';
    const WRAP_NOTE = '{one line the agent mentions while wrapping up}';

    const base44 = createClientFromRequest(req);

    // Today's date where the CLIENT is, as YYYY-MM-DD ('en-CA' formats ISO-style).
    const todayLocal = (): string =>
      new Date().toLocaleDateString('en-CA', { timeZone: TIMEZONE });

    const normaliseDate = (raw: string): string => {
      if (!raw) return '';
      const s = String(raw).trim().toLowerCase();
      const today = todayLocal();
      if (s === 'today' || s === 'now' || s === 'tonight') return today;
      if (s === 'tomorrow' || s === 'next day' || s === 'tmr') {
        return new Date(new Date(today + 'T12:00:00Z').getTime()
          + 24 * 3600 * 1000).toISOString().slice(0, 10);
      }
      const WEEKDAYS = ['sunday','monday','tuesday','wednesday','thursday','friday','saturday'];
      const wd = WEEKDAYS.indexOf(s.replace(/^next /, ''));
      if (wd >= 0) {
        const t = new Date(today + 'T12:00:00Z');
        const diff = ((wd - t.getUTCDay()) + 7) % 7 || 7;
        return new Date(t.getTime() + diff * 24 * 3600 * 1000).toISOString().slice(0, 10);
      }
      if (/^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}/.test(s)) return s.slice(0, 10);
      const parsed = new Date(raw);
      if (!isNaN(parsed.getTime())) {
        const iso = parsed.toISOString().slice(0, 10);
        return iso < today ? '' : iso;
      }
      return '';
    };

    // "9am", "9:00 AM", "midday" -> "09:00"
    const normaliseTime = (raw: string): string => {
      if (!raw) return '';
      const s = String(raw).trim().toLowerCase();
      if (s === 'midday' || s === 'noon') return '12:00';
      const m = s.match(/^(\d{1,2})(?::(\d{2}))?\s*(am|pm)?/);
      if (!m) return '';
      let h = parseInt(m[1], 10);
      const mins = m[2] || '00';
      if (m[3] === 'pm' && h < 12) h += 12;
      if (m[3] === 'am' && h === 12) h = 0;
      if (h < 0 || h > 23) return '';
      return `${String(h).padStart(2, '0')}:${mins}`;
    };

    // "14:00" -> "2pm", because the agent says these out loud
    const spoken = (t: string): string => {
      const h = parseInt(t.slice(0, 2), 10);
      return h === 12 ? '12pm' : h < 12 ? `${h}am` : `${h - 12}pm`;
    };

    const DATE_HELP = "Ask the caller which day, then pass the literal word "
      + "'today' or 'tomorrow', a weekday name, or a YYYY-MM-DD date. "
      + "Never guess dates yourself.";

    if (toolName === 'check_availability') {
      const date = normaliseDate(args.date || args.booking_date || '');
      if (!date) {
        return new Response(JSON.stringify({ success: false,
          message: `Date missing or in the past. ${DATE_HELP}` }),
          { status: 200, headers });
      }
      let bookedTimes: string[] = [];
      try {
        const existing = await base44.asServiceRole.entities.Booking.filter({ date });
        bookedTimes = (existing || [])
          .filter((b: any) => b.status === 'pending' || b.status === 'confirmed')
          .map((b: any) => b.time);
      } catch (e) { bookedTimes = []; }
      const available = SLOTS.filter((s: string) => !bookedTimes.includes(s));
      return new Response(JSON.stringify({
        success: true, date,
        available_slots: available,
        available_slots_spoken: available.map(spoken),
        message: available.length > 0
          ? `Slots open on ${date}: ${available.slice(0, 3).map(spoken).join(', ')}. Offer two or three, not the whole list.`
          : `No slots left on ${date}. Offer the next day.`,
      }), { status: 200, headers });
    }

    if (toolName === 'create_booking') {
      const name = args.name || args.first_name || 'Customer';
      const phone = args.phone || 'caller ID (on call record)';
      const address = args.address || '';
      const date = normaliseDate(args.date || args.booking_date || '');
      const time = normaliseTime(args.time || args.booking_time || '');

      if (!address || !date || !time) {
        return new Response(JSON.stringify({ success: false,
          message: `Booking blocked. Missing or invalid: `
            + `${!address ? 'address ' : ''}${!date ? `date (${DATE_HELP})` : ''}`
            + `${(!date && !time) ? ' and ' : ''}${!time ? 'time' : ''}. `
            + `Get these from the caller, then try once more.` }),
          { status: 200, headers });
      }

      try {
        // Duplicate check: same name, date and time already saved = do not double-book
        let existing: any = null;
        try {
          const dupes = await base44.asServiceRole.entities.Booking.filter({ date, time, name });
          existing = (dupes || [])[0];
        } catch (e) { /* ignore */ }

        if (existing && existing.status === 'confirmed') {
          return new Response(JSON.stringify({
            success: true, booking_id: existing.id, name, date, time,
            message: `Already booked: ${name} on ${date} at ${spoken(time)}, `
              + `${existing.address}. Read it back once and wrap up.`,
          }), { status: 200, headers });
        }

        const created = await base44.asServiceRole.entities.Booking.create({
          name, phone, address,
          service: args.service || DEFAULT_SERVICE,
          date, time, status: 'confirmed',
        });

        return new Response(JSON.stringify({
          success: true, booking_id: created?.id, name, date, time,
          message: `Booked: ${name} on ${date} at ${spoken(time)}, ${address}. `
            + `Read it back once, ${WRAP_NOTE}, then wrap up.`,
        }), { status: 200, headers });
      } catch (e) {
        console.log('create error:', String(e));
        return new Response(JSON.stringify({ success: false,
          message: 'Booking system temporarily unavailable. Do not retry more '
            + 'than once. Say someone will call back to confirm.' }),
          { status: 200, headers });
      }
    }

    return new Response(JSON.stringify({ success: false,
      message: `Unknown tool: ${toolName}` }), { status: 200, headers });
  } catch (e) {
    console.log('endpoint error:', String(e));
    return new Response(JSON.stringify({ success: false,
      message: 'Tool endpoint error.' }), { status: 200, headers });
  }
});
```

## Part 3: the inbound_call_router function

Retell calls this when a call arrives on the number, before the agent starts
speaking. It returns which agent answers and any variables that agent should know.

- Input: Retell's inbound call payload, which includes the caller's number.
- Output: the agent id to use, plus any dynamic variables the greeting needs, for
  example `{BUSINESS_NAME}` and today's date in `{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}`.
- Keep it fast and boring. If it errors, the call fails.

## Part 4: the retell_webhook function

Retell posts call events here: `call_started`, `call_ended` and `call_analyzed`.

For V1, receive all three, verify the signature the same way `booking_api` does,
and log them so the owner can see what happened on a call. `call_analyzed` carries
the post-call analysis fields, which is where the call summary lives.

### CallLog entity, designed now, built later

The design is here so it is ready when the owner wants reporting. Do not build it
unless they ask.

- `call_id` (text) the Retell call id
- `call_date` (text)
- `duration_seconds` (number)
- `caller_phone` (text)
- `outcome` (choice: booking, message, transfer, question, other)
- `summary` (text) from the post-call analysis
- `disconnection_reason` (text)

When it exists, `retell_webhook` writes one row per `call_analyzed` event and the
owner gets per-client monthly reporting for free.

### Reference implementation for the webhook

The canonical shape, reconstructed from the same live build. For V1 without the
CallLog entity, keep the entity write commented out and the console log in: the
function still proves calls are landing. Always return 200, even on errors,
because Retell retries on anything else and a retry storm on a broken handler is
miserable to debug.

```typescript
import { createClientFromRequest } from 'npm:@base44/sdk@0.8.31';

Deno.serve(async (req) => {
  const headers = { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' };
  try {
    const raw = await req.text();
    let event: any = {};
    try { event = JSON.parse(raw); } catch { /* ignore */ }

    const eventType = event.event_type || event.event || '';
    const call = event.call || event;
    const callId = call.call_id || event.call_id || '';
    console.log('retell_webhook event:', eventType, 'call:', callId);

    const base44 = createClientFromRequest(req);

    if (eventType === 'call.ended' || eventType === 'call_analyzed'
        || event.call_analysis || event.post_call_analysis) {
      const analysis = event.post_call_analysis || event.call_analysis || {};
      try {
        // Upsert by call_id: read first, then create or update
        const existing = await base44.asServiceRole.entities.CallLog.filter({ call_id: callId });
        const payload = {
          call_id: callId,
          agent_id: call.agent_id || '',
          direction: call.direction || '',
          from_number: call.from_number || '',
          to_number: call.to_number || '',
          duration_seconds: call.call_cost?.duration_seconds ?? 0,
          disconnection_reason: call.disconnection_reason || '',
          call_summary: analysis.call_summary || '',
          caller_name: analysis.data?.caller_name || '',
          caller_number: analysis.data?.caller_number || call.from_number || '',
          booking_made: analysis.data?.booking_made ?? false,
        };
        if ((existing || []).length > 0) {
          await base44.asServiceRole.entities.CallLog.update(existing[0].id, payload);
        } else {
          await base44.asServiceRole.entities.CallLog.create(payload);
        }
      } catch (e) {
        console.log('CallLog write failed:', String(e));
        // Still return 200 so Retell does not retry-storm
      }
    }

    return new Response(JSON.stringify({ received: true }), { status: 200, headers });
  } catch (e) {
    return new Response(JSON.stringify({ received: true, error: String(e) }), { status: 200, headers });
  }
});
```

## What this V1 does NOT do

- **No SMS.** Do not build, promise or mention a text message to the caller or to
  staff. On an Australian number that needs a Twilio regulatory bundle and a
  messaging permission that are not in place. The Booking entity is the source of
  truth: the owner checks bookings in the app, or asks their co-founder agent for
  the day's bookings. SMS is the first upgrade once the paperwork clears, not part
  of V1. `[VERIFY: SMS dispatch is blocked pending the Twilio regulatory bundle.]`
- **No calendar.** Google Calendar and platform booking APIs are a later tier. The
  entity is the calendar in V1.

## Read-back verification, do this before you say you are finished

Print each of these and compare them to what the owner asked for, one at a time.
Do not summarise. Show the actual values.

1. The Booking entity name and every field name and type.
2. The full live URL of `booking_api`, `inbound_call_router` and `retell_webhook`.
   Read the app subdomain character by character. A wrong URL means every tool the
   agent fires will fail silently and the conversation will sound fine while
   nothing gets saved.
3. `{CLIENT_TIMEZONE}` exactly as it will be used in the date code.
4. The open time, close time and slot length.
5. A live test: call `check_availability` with `date` set to the literal word
   `tomorrow` and show the resolved date it returns. Confirm with the owner that
   this is genuinely tomorrow where the client is.
6. A live test: call `create_booking` with a made-up name, then show the new
   Booking row. Then delete the test row.

Only when all six read back correctly do you tell the owner the backend is ready
for the agent to be built on top of it.

---
name: receptionist-client-intake
description: The interview to run before building an AI receptionist for a real client, and the plan it produces. Works out the country and phone type, the routing mode, the transfer mobile, the forwarding code, the booking situation and the never-say list. Catches the loop trap before it takes a client's phones down. Run it every time a client says yes, and again whenever a client wants to change when the AI picks up.
---

# Receptionist Client Intake

## What this skill is for

Every client already has a phone number. That number stays theirs. Your job is to
get their unanswered calls to reach the AI without breaking anything they rely on.

There are several ways that can work depending on what kind of phone they have and
when they want the AI to pick up. Guessing wastes days and can take a business
offline. This skill asks the right questions first, then writes a plan the owner
can follow without thinking.

## The rules you follow

1. **Never guess.** If you do not know something, write "check this with the owner"
   or "test this before go-live". Never state as fact something you were not told
   and that is not in this skill.
2. **Plain language.** Write so a twelve year old could follow it. Say "the phone
   company", not "the carrier", wherever you can.
3. **One job per step.** Every step has one action and one person doing it.
4. **Say who does it.** Tag every step YOU (the owner running the agency), CLIENT
   (the business owner), or ME (this agent).
5. **Never ring the client's business number back.** See the loop trap. This is the
   fastest way to break a client's phones.
6. **No em-dashes in anything you write.**

---

## Part 1: the interview

Ask these in order, one or two at a time, in normal conversation. Do not move on
without an answer. If the owner does not know, that becomes a question for the
client and you say so.

### Q1. Which country is the client in?

Why it matters: it decides where the number comes from, whether identity paperwork
is needed, and what the forwarding code is.

- United States or Canada: Retell sells the number directly. No Twilio needed.
- Australia, New Zealand, United Kingdom and much of Europe: the number comes from
  Twilio and gets imported into Retell. Identity paperwork usually has to be
  approved first, which takes days, not hours.
- Anywhere else: say plainly "I have not been given the rules for that country. Do
  not promise the client a date until we check what Twilio needs there."

### Q2. What is the business number, and what kind of phone is it?

Read the four options out and let them pick:

- **A. A mobile.** The business number is a phone in someone's pocket.
- **B. A landline or internet phone.** A fixed line at a shop or office, usually a
  handset on a desk, often bundled with the internet.
- **C. A phone system.** More than one handset, or extensions, or someone who
  transfers calls. Names to listen for: 3CX, RingCentral, Aircall, Zoom Phone,
  Microsoft Teams Phone, "our VoIP system", "our PBX".
- **D. They do not know.**

If the answer is D, give the owner these exact words to send the client:

> Quick one so I can set your AI up properly.
> 1. Is your business number a mobile you carry, or a phone sitting at the shop?
> 2. Who do you pay for it each month?
> 3. Does anyone else's phone ring when that number is called?
> 4. When you miss a call now, what happens? Voicemail, or does it ring someone
>    else?

Map the answers: mobile they carry equals A. One handset at the shop, nobody else
rings, equals B. Anyone else rings, or extensions, or a system named, equals C.
Still unclear means ask them to log in wherever they pay the bill and read out what
the service is called. Do not proceed on a guess.

### Q3. Which phone company?

You need the company name to give them the right forwarding code and to know
whether forwarding is even available on their plan.

### Q4. When should the AI pick up? The five modes.

Read these out in plain words and let them choose one.

- **Overflow.** Their phone rings first, like normal. If nobody answers after about
  twenty seconds, the AI takes it. Outside hours it goes straight to the AI. This
  is what most people want and it is the simplest to build.
- **After hours only.** In business hours they answer everything and the AI never
  touches a call. Outside those hours the AI takes everything.
- **Always AI first.** Every call goes to the AI, every time. It handles bookings
  and questions and passes real humans through.
- **AI as backup, with transfer.** Overflow, but the AI also offers to put urgent
  callers through to their mobile.
- **Split by intent.** The AI answers everything, handles the simple calls, and
  passes anything complicated to a human.

These are routing settings, not different agents. The same agent works for all
five. What changes is where the calls come from before they reach the AI.

Recommend Overflow unless they have a reason. It needs one code tapped into one
phone and there is nothing else to break. After hours and Split need a piece of
routing that decides call by call, which is more to build and more to test.

If they choose anything other than Overflow, ask their exact business hours and
which days.

### Q5. What mobile should the AI ring when a caller needs a human?

This must be a **different number from the business number**. Get it in full
international format and read it back digit by digit.

If the owner is still on a Twilio trial account, that mobile has to be verified in
Twilio before the AI can dial it. Do that now, not on the day you go live.

If the client only has one phone, say it plainly:

> Because your business number sends missed calls to the AI, the AI cannot ring
> that same number back or the call goes in a circle. Two options. Either we keep
> it simple and the AI takes a message every time, or you give me a second number
> to reach you on.

### Q6. THE LOOP TRAP CHECK. Ask this directly, every single time.

Ask the client, in these words:

> On the mobile you want the AI to transfer calls to, is there any call forwarding
> switched on right now? Anything that sends calls somewhere else, always or when
> you miss them?

Why this question exists: if the transfer mobile has **unconditional forwarding**
pointing back at the AI number, the AI transfers a caller, the caller lands on the
mobile, the mobile immediately forwards them back to the AI, and round it goes. The
client's phones effectively stop working.

The rule: the transfer mobile must have **no forwarding, or conditional forwarding
only** (forward when not answered, or when busy). Never unconditional.

**Never infer forwarding from call data.** On the live build a diversion marker in
the call information led to a wrong conclusion that the owner had forwarding set up
when he had never touched it. Ask the person. Believe the person. If they are not
sure, have them check with the code in the table below and read the result back to
you.

### Q7. What happens when the AI takes a booking? The booking situation.

Three tiers. Find out which one this client is.

- **Tier 1, the default that always works.** The AI takes all the details and they
  land in the app as a booking record. The owner sees them in the app, or asks
  their co-founder agent for the day's bookings. This works for every business on
  earth, needs nothing from the client, and is what you sell. Most trades are here,
  running on a diary or a whiteboard.
- **Tier 2, Google Calendar.** The client already lives in Google Calendar and
  wants real-time availability and real bookings in it. Possible, needs a bridge
  built and a Google login connected. Only offer it if they already use Google
  Calendar as their actual calendar.
- **Tier 3, their booking platform.** Some platforms let other software book into
  them directly. More work, higher price, and only worth it for a client whose
  whole business runs through one.

The honest script when a client has an unusual system:

> We work with all sorts of systems. Either we connect straight to yours, or the AI
> qualifies the caller and takes the full booking, which lands in one place you
> check. During setup we test your exact workflow. If we cannot make it seamless,
> you do not pay.

Do not promise a text message to the caller or to staff. That needs paperwork on
the phone number that is not in place yet. `[VERIFY: SMS is blocked pending the
Twilio regulatory bundle and messaging permission. It is the first upgrade, not
part of the first build.]`

### Q8. Who is the emergency contact, and what counts as an emergency?

For a trade, a burst pipe at 11pm is not the same call as a quote request. Get the
number, and get the client's own words for what counts.

### Q9. What should the AI never say?

Prices they do not want quoted. Timeframes they cannot hold to. Anything about
warranties, insurance or someone else's work. Write it down word for word. It goes
straight into the agent's guardrails.

### Q10. Whose name is the phone account in?

The normal answer is that the Twilio account sits in the client's business name and
the client pays Twilio directly, and the owner has the login and manages it. That
keeps the number portable and keeps the owner out of the billing.

---

## Part 2: the decision tree

Work through this in order. Do not skip ahead and do not invent a branch.

### Branch 1: the country

```
United States or Canada?
  YES -> Retell sells the number directly. No Twilio, no paperwork.
         Buy the number in Retell, bind the agent, then go to Branch 2.
  NO  -> You need a Twilio number. Continue.

Australia, New Zealand, United Kingdom or Europe?
  YES -> Twilio sells numbers there, and each one needs identity paperwork
         approved before you can buy it. Once one bundle is approved it covers
         every future number of the same type in the same country.
         If the paperwork is already approved: the number can be live today.
         If it is not: tell the client "later this week", never "this afternoon".
  NO  -> Stop. Tell the owner you have not been given the rules for that country.
```

### Branch 2: where the client's number lives

```
A. A MOBILE on a normal mobile company
   -> Nothing to build. The CLIENT taps one code into their own phone, once.
      Twenty seconds. Go to the forwarding codes below.

B. A LANDLINE or INTERNET PHONE
   -> Codes usually do not work. Forwarding is a setting on the phone company's
      website. The CLIENT logs in, or adds YOU as a user.
      Ask: "Can you log in to your phone service online? Who do you pay?"
      Then say honestly: "I do not know that provider's exact menu. Get on a
      screen share and look for Call Forward, Divert or Follow Me. The setting
      you want is forward when not answered, pointing at the new number."
      The usual hold-up is that nobody knows the login. Ask for it a day early.

C. A PHONE SYSTEM (3CX, RingCentral, Aircall, Teams Phone and similar)
   -> Someone already looks after it. YOU do not touch it. Tell their phone
      person what you need. Give the owner this to send:
        "Hi, we are adding an AI receptionist. Can you add a forward rule so that
        when a call is not answered after about twenty seconds it goes to
        {TWILIO_NUMBER}? Please leave everything else as it is."
      Ask what happens today first. These systems often already overflow to
      somebody's mobile, and you do not want two hops.

D. THEY ASK TO MOVE THE NUMBER TO TWILIO
   -> Say no, and say why: "We do not need to move your number. We just point
      missed calls at the new one. Moving it takes weeks, needs signed forms,
      and if anything goes wrong your phone stops working."

E. SOMEONE SUGGESTS HOSTED NUMBERS
   -> They do not do voice calls. Do not offer it for a receptionist.
```

### Branch 3: how the AI gets the call once it reaches Twilio

```
Overflow or Always AI?
  -> Simplest setup. The Twilio number sits on the SIP trunk pointing at Retell,
     and the number is imported into Retell. Nothing else. Recommend this.

After hours or Split by intent?
  -> The simple setup cannot do this. A number attached to a trunk ignores every
     other setting on that number, so there is nowhere to put a clock.
     For these two the number does NOT go on the trunk. It points at a small piece
     of routing that decides, call by call, whether to ring a human or hand the
     call to the AI. Build that routing, then test it with real calls before you
     tell the client it is live.
     [VERIFY: the routing function path has not been proven end to end on a real
     call in this build.]
```

---

## The forwarding codes

These are the codes the CLIENT taps into their own mobile. They are a phone network
standard rather than a Twilio feature, and the same codes are used across
Australian, New Zealand and UK mobile networks.

| What you want | Code to tap | Undo it | Check it |
|---|---|---|---|
| Forward only when they do not answer | `**61*NUMBER#` | `##61#` | `*#61#` |
| Same, choosing how long it rings first | `**61*NUMBER**SECONDS#` | `##61#` | `*#61#` |
| Forward every call, always | `**21*NUMBER#` | `##21#` | `*#21#` |
| Forward when the line is busy | `**67*NUMBER#` | `##67#` | `*#67#` |
| Forward when the phone is off | `**62*NUMBER#` | `##62#` | `*#62#` |
| Undo everything, the panic button | `##002#` | | |

**Confirm the exact code on the client's own phone company support page before you
send it.** Companies change these and some plans do not allow forwarding at all.
United States carriers use different codes again, so look theirs up rather than
using this table. `[VERIFY: these codes have not been live-tested on every
network.]`

Ring time: five to thirty seconds, in steps of five. Roughly five seconds is one
ring. About twenty-five seconds, which is five rings, is the good default.

Always write the number in full international format starting with the plus sign.

### The two 61s that confuse everyone

An Australian code looks like this:

```
**61*+61XXXXXXXXX**25#
```

Two 61s, and they mean completely different things.

- The **first 61**, right after the two stars, is the phone network's code for
  "when I do not answer". It is the same in Australia, New Zealand and the UK and
  has nothing to do with Australia.
- The **second 61**, after the plus sign, is Australia's country code. It is part
  of the phone number.

Say this out loud to the client so they do not think they typed it twice. It looks
like a typo and it is not.

### If the code does not work

- Some phone companies want you to use the phone's own menu instead: Phone app,
  then Settings, then Call forwarding, then "when unanswered", then the number.
- Some prepaid plans cannot forward at all. Check whether they are on prepaid.
- If nothing works, have them tap `##002#` to clear everything and start again.

### The cost question, answered honestly

The client's phone company may charge them for the forwarded leg, as if they had
made that call themselves. On some plans it is free. Say this word for word:

> Your phone company might charge you for the diverted leg like a call you made
> yourself. On a lot of plans it is included. Have a quick look at whether your
> plan has unlimited standard calls. If it does, you will not notice it.

Never promise it is free.

---

## The voicemail trap

This one comes free with every setup that rings a human.

You ring the owner's mobile to pass a caller through. The owner does not pick up.
Voicemail does. Voicemail counts as someone answering, so the caller gets connected
to a beep and a greeting instead of getting help, everyone hangs up, and from the
outside it looks like it worked.

The fix, built into the routing: before connecting the caller, the system whispers
privately to the owner, "New call for your business, press 1 to take it."
Voicemail cannot press 1. If nothing is pressed within a few seconds, that leg is
dropped and the caller goes to the AI instead. The caller hears normal ringing the
whole time and never knows.

Include this in the plan every time the setup rings a human.

---

## Part 3: write the plan

Output exactly these seven sections, in this order, with these headings. Nothing
else. Plain language throughout.

### 1. What we are doing, in one paragraph

Two or three sentences. Name the client, say their number stays where it is, say
what happens when they miss a call, and say when the AI picks up.

### 2. The steps

A numbered list. Every step tagged YOU, CLIENT or ME, one action each. The shape:

```
1. YOU: Get the identity paperwork approved on the Twilio account. One time job,
   covers every client after this.
2. ME: Buy the number and build the trunk.
3. ME: Connect the number to Retell and put this client's agent on it.
4. YOU: Ring the new number yourself. Confirm the AI answers.
5. CLIENT: On the business phone, tap the forwarding code and press call.
6. YOU: Ring the business number from a different phone, let it ring out, confirm
   the AI answers.
```

### 3. The exact code or setting

The literal string to tap, or the literal path through the menu, written so it can
be pasted into a text message to the client. Add the two 61s explanation if it is
one of those codes. Add the line telling them to confirm it on their phone
company's own support page.

### 4. What it costs to run

The number is a few dollars a month. The voice is charged per minute of call time.
Give the owner the real current figures from Retell and Twilio, say plainly which
currency they are in, and never quote what the client's own phone company charges
for a diverted call.

### 5. Who does what, at a glance

Three short lists: YOU, CLIENT, ME. Headlines only. This is what the owner forwards
to the client.

### 6. Test before you tell the client it is live

Always include these, in this order:

```
1. Ring the new number from your own phone. The AI answers.
2. Ring the client's business number from a phone that is not theirs. Let it ring
   right out. The AI picks up.
3. Ring in and book something. Check the booking appears in the app with the right
   date.
4. Ring the business number and have the CLIENT answer it. Confirm the AI stays out
   of the way and nothing sounds different.
5. Ring in and ask for a human. The call must end as a transfer AND the owner's
   mobile must actually ring.
6. If a human is in the routing: let their voicemail pick up. The caller must end
   up with the AI, not with voicemail.
7. Have the client tap the check code on their phone. It should read back the new
   number.
```

The full detail of steps 1, 3 and 5 lives in `receptionist-test-protocol`. Run that
skill, do not rewrite it here.

### 7. If it goes wrong

Always include these four:

```
- Client wants everything back to normal right now: they tap ##002# and press
  call. Every divert clears in one second and their phone works exactly as before.
  Tell them this line at the start. It takes all the fear out of it.
- The AI never picks up: check the divert is actually on with the check code, then
  ring the new number directly to find out whether the problem is the divert or
  the AI.
- Callers hear an error message: the routing did not answer in time. There should
  be a backup on the number that takes a message instead.
- The AI cannot ring the owner out: outbound dialling to that country is switched
  off in Twilio. Turn it on in voice geographic permissions.
```

---

## Read-back verification

Before you hand the plan over, print and confirm with the owner:

1. Country, phone company, and phone type.
2. The chosen routing mode, in the client's own words.
3. The business hours, if the mode needs them.
4. The transfer mobile, digit by digit, and confirmation that it is NOT the
   business number.
5. The loop trap answer, in the client's own words, and confirmation that any
   forwarding on the transfer mobile is conditional or absent.
6. The booking tier.
7. The emergency contact.
8. The never-say list, word for word.

## Things you never do

- Never tell a client to move their number in order to get started.
- Never ring the client's business number from the routing.
- Never promise the client their phone company will not charge them.
- Never promise a same-day go-live before the identity paperwork is approved.
- Never promise a text message to the caller or to staff in the first build.
- Never put a number on a SIP trunk and then promise time-of-day routing on it.
- Never assume what forwarding a client has. Ask them.
- Never use an em-dash.

---
name: receptionist-test-protocol
description: The four-step test that decides whether an AI receptionist is actually working. Run it in this order on every build, before the owner tells a client anything is live, and again after any change to the agent, the number or the backend.
---

# Receptionist Test Protocol

## What this skill is for

"It sounded great in the test" is not working. A build is working when all four
steps below pass, in this order, with the exact success signals written next to
them. Anything less and the owner is about to tell a client something that is not
true.

Run the whole protocol on every new build. Run it again after any change to the
agent, the phone number, the trunk or the booking backend.

## Parameters

- `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` the business line, full international format.
- `{OWN_MOBILE}` the mobile the transfer goes to, full international format. On a
  Twilio trial account this must be a verified number.
- `{AGENT_ID}` the published agent.
- `{APP_URL}` the Base44 app, for checking the Booking entity.

## Step 1: the dashboard web call

Open the Retell dashboard and press the Test button to talk to the agent in the
browser.

What you are testing: does it hold a conversation, does the booking flow work, do
the tools actually fire.

Run a full booking out loud. Give a name, a phone number, an address, say what you
need, and ask for tomorrow. Listen for:

- It asks one question at a time. If it interrogates you with three at once, the
  role prompt is too long or the facts are in the prompt instead of the knowledge
  base.
- It quotes prices that match the client's website. If it invents one, the
  knowledge base is wrong or missing.
- It offers real times, and the date it reads back is genuinely tomorrow. If it
  says a date from a different year, the date rule is not in the tool descriptions.

**Success signal:** the conversation feels normal, and both tool calls show as
executed in the call detail, not failed.

**What this step can NEVER test:** the transfer. Retell's transfer works on phone
calls only. The dashboard Test button is a web call. Pressing it a hundred times
proves nothing about transfers.

Two practical notes:

- **Use a decent microphone**, especially for a client demo. On a noisy laptop mic
  the speech recognition mangles words. On the live build "callout fee" came
  through as "full outfit".
- **Never host a custom web-call test page on a Base44 function.** Base44 functions
  serve HTML with a content security policy that blocks all scripts, so the page is
  dead in the browser, and Retell refuses web calls from a local file. Use the
  dashboard, or host the page on a real site outside Base44 functions.

## Step 2: the real inbound call

Ring `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` from your own phone.

Run a full booking again, properly, as if you were a customer.

**Success signals, all three:**

1. The agent picks up with the right business greeting.
2. A new row appears in the Booking entity in the app, with the right name, phone,
   address, date and time. Check the date is real, not two years ago.
3. The call events show up from the webhook: call started, call ended, and the
   post-call analysis.

If the conversation was fine but no row appeared, the tool URLs are wrong. Read
them back off the LLM object character by character against the live function URL.

## Step 3: the transfer test

This is the step that gets skipped and it is the one that fails on real clients.

Two ways to run it. Either works.

**Option A, the simple one.** Ring `{TWILIO_NUMBER}`, and part way through say
"can you put me through to a human".

**Option B, placed by API.** Create a phone call through Retell with the business
number as the from number, `{OWN_MOBILE}` as the to number, and `{AGENT_ID}` as the
agent override. Then ask for a human on that call.

**Success signal, and it has two halves. Both must happen:**

1. The call ends with a disconnection reason of `call_transfer`.
2. **Your own mobile rings.**

If the call ends with `call_transfer` but nothing rings, it is not working. If you
hear the error "Transfer call cannot be completed, probably don't have the
permission to perform the transfer", that is almost never about permissions. Go to
the troubleshooting tree in `retell-number-connect` and work the four checks in
order, starting with the termination URI character by character.

## Step 4: check the records after every call

After each test call, open the app and Retell and confirm:

- The Booking row exists and every field is right, especially the date.
- The post-call analysis fields are populated: caller name, what they wanted,
  whether a booking was made.
- The call transcript reads like a conversation a customer would be happy with.

Then delete the test bookings so the client's data starts clean.

## What "working" means, in one line

The agent answered a real call on the real number, a real booking row exists with
the right date, and asking for a human made a real mobile ring. Until all three of
those have happened, the build is not finished, whatever the dashboard says.

## Read-back verification

Print a short pass or fail line for each of the four steps, with the actual
evidence next to it: the greeting you heard, the booking row id, the disconnection
reason, and whether the mobile rang. If any line is a fail, say so plainly and name
which skill fixes it. Do not report a build as ready with a fail on the sheet.

---
name: retell-number-connect
description: Connects a Twilio number to a Retell agent. Imports the number as custom telephony with the exact termination URI, binds the inbound agent, sets the inbound webhook, reads the whole config back, and carries the troubleshooting tree for transfers that fail with a permissions error.
---

# Retell Number Connect

## What this skill is for

The Twilio side is built. This skill joins it to the voice agent, and it is the
place where one wrong character silently breaks transfers.

Read the gotcha below before you do anything else, because it changes the order you
work in.

## Parameters

- `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` in full international format, from `twilio-number-setup`.
- `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}` the trunk's SIP domain, exactly as Twilio shows it.
- `{ACCOUNT_SID}` and `{AUTH_TOKEN}` the Twilio credentials.
- `{AGENT_ID}` the published Retell agent from `receptionist-agent-builder`.
- `{INBOUND_ROUTER_URL}` the live `inbound_call_router` function URL.
- `{NICKNAME}` a name for this number in Retell, for example
  `{BUSINESS_NAME} main line`.
- `{RETELL_API_KEY}` the owner's Retell key.

## THE GOTCHA, read this first

**The Retell update-phone-number call silently ignores changes to the outbound
trunk config.** It returns success. It updates the modification timestamp. It
changes nothing.

The termination URI can only be set at import. So:

- If the termination URI is wrong, you **delete the number in Retell and import it
  again** with the correct value.
- You **never** try to fix a termination URI with an update or a patch. It will
  look like it worked and it will not have worked, and you will spend the next hour
  debugging the wrong thing.

Deleting the number in Retell does not give up the number. Twilio still owns it.
You are only removing Retell's record of it, and you rebuild that record in one
call. What you must restore after re-importing: the inbound agent, the inbound
webhook and the nickname.

## Step 1: import the number

Import `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` into Retell as **custom telephony** with:

- `termination_uri` set to **exactly `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}`**. Not a shortened version.
  Not one with a hyphen or a digit dropped. Character for character.
- Transport: **TCP**.
- The Twilio account SID and auth token, so Retell can place outbound legs.
- `nickname`: `{NICKNAME}`.

Before you send the import, print the termination URI you are about to use on its
own line and compare it to `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}` character by character. This is a ten
second check that prevents the worst bug in this whole build.

## Step 2: bind the inbound agent

Set the inbound agent on the number to `{AGENT_ID}`.

If the owner is running more than one agent version, bind the **published** one,
and check the version number, not just the name.

## Step 3: set the inbound webhook

Set the number's inbound webhook to `{INBOUND_ROUTER_URL}`.

This is what Retell calls when a call arrives, before the agent speaks, and it is
what lets the router hand the agent per-call variables.

## Step 4: read the whole config back and compare it to intent

Fetch the phone number record from Retell and print every field. Then go through
this list line by line:

1. The phone number, digit by digit against `{TWILIO_NUMBER}`.
2. **The termination URI, character by character against `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}`.** Say
   each character out loud. Do not skim it. Do not trust that it looks right.
3. Transport reads TCP.
4. The inbound agent id matches `{AGENT_ID}`, and it is the published version.
5. The inbound webhook matches `{INBOUND_ROUTER_URL}`.
6. The nickname is set.

If any of lines 1 to 3 is wrong, go to the triage tree. Do not patch it.

## The troubleshooting tree: transfer fails with a permissions error

The exact error is: `Transfer call cannot be completed, probably don't have the
permission to perform the transfer.`

Ignore the word "permission". It is almost never about permissions. Work through
these four checks **in this order** and stop at the first one that is wrong.

### Check 1: the termination URI, character by character

Fetch the number record from Retell. Fetch the trunk from Twilio. Put the
termination URI and the trunk domain next to each other and compare them one
character at a time.

This is the cause most of the time. On the live build the trunk domain ended in
`-001` and the imported termination URI did not, so every transfer was dialling a
trunk that does not exist. Nothing in the error message hinted at that.

If they do not match, go to Check 4.

### Check 2: the trunk transfer mode

Read the trunk's transfer mode from Twilio. It must be `enable-all`. If it is
anything else, set it to `enable-all` and test again. Transfers over SIP need it
and it is not on by default.

### Check 3: the IP access control list

Read the trunk's authentication. There must be an IP access control list attached
containing `18.98.16.120/30`, which is the Retell SBC block. If the trunk has no
credentials and no IP list, Twilio rejects Retell's SIP and the call goes nowhere.
Attach the list and test again.

Also confirm outbound dialling to the country is switched on in Twilio's voice
geographic permissions, and on a trial account, that the transfer target is a
verified number.

### Check 4: the termination URI is wrong, so re-import

Not a patch. A delete and re-import.

1. Read the current config and write down the inbound agent id, the inbound webhook
   URL and the nickname. You are about to lose all three.
2. Delete the number in Retell.
3. Import it again with `termination_uri` set to the **correct** `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}`,
   transport TCP.
4. Restore the inbound agent.
5. Restore the inbound webhook.
6. Restore the nickname.
7. Read the whole config back again, character by character.
8. Test the transfer on a real call, not a web call.

## Two limits that will waste your afternoon if you forget them

- **A web call can never test a transfer.** Retell's transfer only works on phone
  calls. The dashboard Test button is a web call, so it will never prove a transfer
  works no matter how many times you press it.
- **A green dashboard test is not a working phone line.** The only proof is a real
  call to the real number. See `receptionist-test-protocol`.

## Read-back verification before you say the number is connected

Print all six lines from Step 4 again, plus:

7. The trunk transfer mode, read from Twilio, showing `enable-all`.
8. The IP access control list contents, showing `18.98.16.120/30`.
9. The origination URL on the trunk, showing `sip:sip.retellai.com`.

Then hand over to `receptionist-test-protocol` and run steps 2 and 3, the real
inbound call and the transfer test.

---
name: twilio-number-setup
description: Sets up the phone side of an AI receptionist in Twilio. Saves the account credentials correctly, buys the local number, builds the elastic SIP trunk with the settings that make transfers work, turns on the right dialling permissions, and reads every value back before handing over to Retell.
---

# Twilio Number Setup

## What this skill is for

Retell sells phone numbers in the United States and Canada only. Everywhere else,
the number comes from Twilio and gets connected to Retell over SIP. This skill
builds the Twilio side and hands one exact value to the next skill: the trunk
domain.

Get one character of that domain wrong and every transfer fails with an error
message about permissions that has nothing to do with permissions. That happened on
the live build and it cost a full day. This skill exists so it does not happen
again.

## Parameters

- `{COUNTRY}` and `{AREA_CODE}` where the client's number should look local.
- `{ACCOUNT_SID}` the Twilio Account SID.
- `{AUTH_TOKEN}` the Twilio Auth Token.
- `{TRUNK_NAME}` a name for the trunk, unique to this client.
- `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}` the trunk's SIP domain, which Twilio generates. You do not
  choose the format, you read it back exactly as shown.
- `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` the number you buy, in full international format.

## The trial account, said plainly up front

A brand new Twilio account is a trial account, and a trial account is enough to
build and test the whole thing yourself. Tell the owner this before they reach for a
credit card.

What a trial account gives you:

- One elastic SIP trunk. One is all this build needs.
- Outbound calls only to **verified** numbers. The owner verifies their own mobile
  in Twilio, and that same mobile is the transfer target while testing. It lines up
  exactly.

What it will not do:

- A second trunk. Trying returns error `21244`, "Only one trunk allowed for trial
