  accounts".
- Outbound to a number that is not verified.

The upgrade happens at the first paying client, and the client's money pays for it.
Do not tell someone to upgrade before they have one.

Buying a local number in some countries also needs identity paperwork approved
first, which is a separate wait. `[VERIFY: confirm the paperwork requirement for
{COUNTRY} on Twilio's own regulatory guidelines page before promising a go-live
date.]`

## Step 1: credentials, and the five second check that saves an hour

The owner needs the **Account SID** and the **Auth Token** from the Twilio console
home page. Save both into the app's secrets.

**Use the Auth Token. Never use a scoped API key.** Scoped keys hit permission walls
on trunking and on messaging, and every wall looks like a different bug. The Auth
Token has full account access and skips all of it.

Then run this check before you make a single API call:

- An Account SID starts with `AC` and is **34 characters** long.
- An Auth Token is **32 characters** long and does not start with `AC`.

If both values start with `AC`, the same value got pasted twice, or an automatic
secret detector grabbed the wrong line. That is exactly what happened on the live
build, and every Twilio call came back with error `20003 Authenticate` while both
values looked correct on screen. Count the characters. Then make one harmless read
call and confirm it returns the account.

## Step 2: buy the number

- Search Twilio's available local numbers for `{COUNTRY}` with `{AREA_CODE}`.
- Buy one that has **Voice** capability.
- Record `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` in full international format, starting with the plus
  sign.

The number costs a few dollars a month. On a real client the Twilio account sits in
the client's business name and the client pays Twilio directly, so this line never
comes out of the owner's pocket.

## Step 3: create the elastic SIP trunk

Create an elastic SIP trunk named `{TRUNK_NAME}` and set exactly these:

- **Transfer mode: `enable-all`.** Without this, a transfer on a real call fails.
  This is not optional and it is not a default.
- **Authentication: IP access control list.** Create an IP access control list
  containing the Retell SBC block `18.98.16.120/30` and attach it to the trunk.
- **No SIP credentials.** The IP list is the whole auth story. Credentials are one
  more secret to manage, leak and rotate. Do not create any.
- **Origination URL: `sip:sip.retellai.com`**, priority 1, weight 10, enabled.
  This is the address Twilio sends incoming calls to, and it is the same for
  everyone.
- **Secure: off.**
- Caller name lookup: off.
- Recording: do not record at the trunk. Retell handles recording.

Then attach `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` to the trunk.

One consequence to know before you attach it: a number attached to a trunk ignores
every other voice setting on that number. That is fine for the overflow and
always-AI routing modes, which is what this build uses. Time-of-day routing needs a
different setup that does not go through the trunk.

## Step 4: note the trunk domain, exactly as shown

Twilio generates a SIP domain for the trunk. It looks like
`something.pstn.twilio.com`.

**Copy it exactly. Do not type it from memory. Do not shorten it. Do not tidy it
up.** On the live build the real domain ended in `-001` and the version typed into
Retell did not, and every transfer failed for a day with an error about permissions.

Read it back to the owner character by character, including any digits or hyphens,
and save it as `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}`.

## Step 5: dialling permissions

Turn on outbound voice dialling to `{COUNTRY}` in Twilio's voice geographic
permissions. Without it, the agent cannot dial out to transfer a caller, and the
failure looks like a Retell problem when it is a Twilio setting.

On a trial account, also verify the owner's own mobile as a caller ID, because
that is the only number the account is allowed to dial while testing.

## Step 6: read every value back

Print these, one per line, and compare each against the console with the owner
watching:

1. `{ACCOUNT_SID}` first four and last four characters, and its length. Should be
   34, starting `AC`.
2. Auth token length. Should be 32.
3. `{TWILIO_NUMBER}` in full international format, digit by digit.
4. The trunk name and trunk SID.
5. **`{TRUNK_DOMAIN}`, read character by character.** Then have the owner read the
   domain off the Twilio screen out loud while you compare. This one value is the
   whole reason this section exists.
6. Trunk transfer mode. Must read `enable-all`.
7. The IP access control list, and the block inside it. Must read
   `18.98.16.120/30`.
8. The origination URL. Must read `sip:sip.retellai.com`.
9. That the number is attached to the trunk.
10. That outbound dialling to `{COUNTRY}` is enabled.
11. On a trial account, that the owner's mobile shows as a verified number.

Only when all eleven read back correctly do you hand `{TRUNK_DOMAIN}` and
`{TWILIO_NUMBER}` to `retell-number-connect`.

## Things you never do

- Never create a scoped API key for this build.
- Never create SIP credentials when an IP access control list will do.
- Never retype the trunk domain from memory.
- Never tell someone to upgrade Twilio before they have a paying client.
- Never promise a go-live date before the country's identity paperwork is approved.

---
name: property-walkthrough-blueprint
description: The complete blueprint of the Property Walkthrough app, the seven-step student wizard that turns real estate listing photos into one sellable AI video walkthrough. Read this before building or changing anything in that app, and follow it exactly, piece by piece.
---

# Property Walkthrough, the full blueprint

## Rules

1. Never build the whole app in one go. Build only the piece the user names. If the user says "build the app", ask which piece, or start at Piece 1 and stop.
2. If a request conflicts with this blueprint, follow the blueprint and say so plainly.
3. These things belong to the student. Nothing automatic may ever overwrite them: their photo order, their location labels (including custom labels typed via Other), their camera movement choices, their movement guidance text, their quality-control verdicts (Approved, Retry, Flagged), their uploaded clip files, their final video URL, and their listing details.
4. Never create records during a build. No sample data, no test listings, no demo photos. The shape only. The app is finished when a student creates their own real listing.
5. Never delete a photo or listing outright. Archival only, as described below.

## What this app is

A Base44 app a student uses to run a property walkthrough video service. The student uploads a real estate agent's listing photos, orders them, labels them, and the app generates one Envato-ready Kling 3.0 prompt per photo pair. The student generates the clips on Envato's website, uploads them back, quality-controls them, and finishes one final stitched walkthrough video to sell to the agent. The video generation itself happens outside the app. The app is the factory desk: ordering, labeling, prompt generation, quality control, and delivery.

## The pieces, in build order

### Piece 1. The Listing entity

One listing is one property, one job. Fields:

- address (string, the full address)
- agent_name (string)
- agent_phone (string)
- agent_agency (string)
- price (string, like "$850,000")
- description (string, the listing's selling points)
- bedrooms (number)
- bathrooms (number)
- car_spaces (number)
- notes (string, the student's own notes)
- status (string, a stage label, not a workflow gate. Exactly these values, in this order: Images Pending, Images Uploaded, Prompts Ready, Stitched. Default: Images Pending)
- final_video_url (string, empty until the finished video exists)
- order_confirmed (boolean, default false. True once the student confirms the photo route)

Auto-flip rules, the app sets these, never the student:
- First photo uploaded on a listing: status becomes Images Uploaded.
- Every scene on the listing has a generated prompt: status becomes Prompts Ready.
- final_video_url gets a value: status becomes Stitched.

Edge cases:
- Status only moves forward. A student relabeling photos after confirm must not drop the status back to Images Pending. Order changes are handled by the stale system in Piece 6, not by status.
- Do not add statuses like Delivered, Called or Yes. This app is the video factory, not the lead CRM.

### Piece 2. The ListingPhoto entity

One photo in the walking order. Fields:

- listing_id (reference to Listing)
- image_url (string, the hosted photo file)
- source_filename (string, the original filename the student uploaded, kept exactly as uploaded)
- sequence_order (number, 1-based position in the walk)
- location (string, the student's room label, like "Kitchen". Empty until labeled)
- next_instruction (string, OPTIONAL. The student's extra movement guidance for the transition that starts at this photo, like "Move through the sliding doors toward the outdoor area". Blank is normal and fine)
- archived (boolean, default false. The only form of deletion. When a photo is archived: archived = true and sequence_order = 0, and the remaining photos renumber so there are no gaps)

Edge cases:
- Never hard-delete a photo. The archive button is the only remove action anywhere in the app.
- A photo with sequence_order 0 or archived true is invisible to the route, the confirm logic and the breadcrumb. This is the rule that prevents the archived-photo bug described in Piece 7.
- Uploaded photos auto-number to the end of the current order.

### Piece 3. The Transition entity

One transition is one video scene: the move from photo N to photo N+1. N photos in order make N minus 1 transitions. Fields:

- listing_id (reference to Listing)
- sequence_order (number, 1-based, matches the pair's position in the walk)
- start_photo (reference to ListingPhoto, anchored by ID, never by position)
- end_photo (reference to ListingPhoto, anchored by ID)
- start_location (string, copied from the start photo's location at confirm time)
- end_location (string, copied from the end photo's location at confirm time)
- student_instruction (string, copied from the start photo's next_instruction at confirm time. May be blank)
- camera_movement (string, one of exactly these 8 options, default "Push forward": Push forward, Move through doorway, Move down hallway, Turn around corner, Reveal adjoining room, Move outside, Pull back, Clean cut)
- generated_prompt (string, the Envato-ready prompt, built by Piece 5)
- clip_filename (string, canonical, built at confirm time: two-digit sequence number, then start location, then "to", then end location, lowercase, underscores, no spaces, like 01_front_facade_to_entrance.mp4)
- result_video_url (string, empty until the student uploads the finished clip for this scene)
- qc_status (string enum: Pending, Accepted, Retry, Flagged. Default Pending)
- is_stale (boolean, default false. True means the photo order changed after confirm and this scene must be re-confirmed)

Edge cases:
- Locations are copied at confirm time, not live-linked. If the student edits a photo label after confirm, the fix is: mark scenes stale, re-confirm. Same route, fresh copies.
- Rebuilding transitions deletes and recreates them, which wipes generated prompts on purpose. The student re-runs prompt generation after re-confirming. That is the intended cost of reordering, and the stale badge warns them first.
- The UI must hide these words: Transition, ListingPhoto, sequence_order, qc_status, is_stale, archived. Plain language everywhere, see Piece 14.

### Piece 4. The app shell

The frame everything else mounts into.

- A listings dashboard: one card per listing with the address and its status. A "New Listing" button. Clicking a card opens that listing's wizard. Empty state: one line, "Add your first listing."
- A new-listing form: address, agent name, phone, agency, price, bedrooms, bathrooms, car spaces, description. Creates the listing at status Images Pending and opens its wizard.
- Inside a listing, a seven-step wizard with a visible progress bar across the top, seven ticks in this order: Photos, Order, Labels, Route, Envato, Clips, Finished. The current step is highlighted. Completed ticks are filled.
- Progress saves after every action. Reloading the page returns the student to their listing in the state they left it.
- Steps that are not ready yet show why in one plain sentence, like "Upload photos to start." They never show a dead button without a reason.

### Piece 5. Step 1, Upload photos

- Multiple photo upload at once. Large thumbnails immediately. Original filenames saved to source_filename.
- Photos append to the end of the order. First photo flips the listing to Images Uploaded.
- Each photo can be archived from this step too (the archive rule from Piece 2, same behavior everywhere).
- Do not auto-label, auto-describe, or auto-detect anything. No AI looks at these photos. This was tried and removed. The student is the only source of truth for what a photo shows.

### Piece 6. Step 2, Put photos in walking order

- Large drag-and-drop cards with obvious drag handles. Works on desktop pointer and touch. Move-left and move-right buttons on every card as the fallback.
- Cards display big position numbers: 01, 02, 03.
- One instruction line at the top: "Put these in the order a buyer would walk through the property."
- Reordering renumbers with no gaps, 1-based.
- If order_confirmed is true and the student reorders: every scene on the listing is marked stale (is_stale = true, prompts preserved not wiped), and the wizard requires the route to be re-confirmed before Envato generation and clip upload count as valid. A plain-language notice explains this: "You changed the order. Review and confirm the route again."
- No database words on screen. Never show sequence_order or stale.

### Piece 7. Step 3, Label each image

- Every photo gets quick-pick chips, exactly these, in this order: Exterior, Front door, Entry, Living room, Kitchen, Dining room, Bedroom, Bathroom, Balcony, Hallway, Other.
- Selecting Other immediately shows a required text field: "What is this room or area?" The typed value becomes the photo's location, and it is kept as typed. Chips never rewrite a custom label.
- The student can change any label any time by tapping another chip. Their choice is never auto-changed by anything.
- Every visible photo must have a location before the student can move to Step 4. If any are blank, Step 4 shows inline: which photo number, which photo, "Label this photo to continue." No modal errors, no silent blocking.
- CRITICAL edge case, this exact bug shipped once: validation must only check NON-ARCHIVED photos. An archived photo with sequence_order 0 and no location must never block confirming the route. If confirm ever fails with a missing-fields error while every visible photo is labeled, this is the bug, and the fix is filtering archived photos out of validation.

### Piece 8. Step 4, Review the route, and the confirm function

The confirm action is a backend function. Build it exactly like this.

Function: createTransitionsForListing. Input: listing_id.
Behavior:
1. Read all photos where archived is false, ordered by sequence_order.
2. If fewer than two photos: fail with "Add at least two photos first."
3. If any of those photos has an empty location: fail with a clear error that names the photo's position and ID, like "Missing location on photo 4". Nothing is built on a failed confirm.
4. Delete the listing's existing transitions and rebuild them: one per consecutive pair, anchored start_photo and end_photo by ID, copying locations and the start photo's next_instruction into student_instruction, setting camera_movement to Push forward, building the canonical clip_filename, is_stale false, qc_status Pending.
5. Set the listing's order_confirmed to true. Return success and the scene count.

The Step 4 screen shows:
- A breadcrumb of the route, like: Exterior, Front door, Entry, Living room, Kitchen.
- Each scene as start image, then an arrow, then end image.
- The movement dropdown on each scene, the 8 options from Piece 3, default Push forward. The student changes movement only when needed.
- An optional "Add movement guidance" text field per scene, saved into the start photo's next_instruction.
- A Confirm route button that calls the function. On success the student moves to Step 5. On failure, the inline missing-photo message from Piece 7 shows. Never a modal, never a silent nothing.

Edge case: re-confirm after reorder keeps the same photos but rebuilds the scenes fresh, so renumbered pairs get correct filenames. Confirming twice in a row without changes is harmless.

### Piece 9. Step 5, the Envato pack, and the prompt function

The prompt generation is a backend function. Deterministic. No AI, no vision, no credits, instant. Build it exactly like this.

Function: generatePromptsForListing. Input: listing_id.
Behavior: for each transition in sequence order, build generated_prompt from this exact template, filling the bracketed parts:

"[Movement option]. Camera rigidly locked on professional tripod, no walking bounce, no vertical bobbing, no micro-jitter, gimbal-smooth only, constant linear speed. In one continuous motion, the camera [movement verb phrase] from [start_location] to [end_location]. [Student instruction, if not blank]. Preserve the fixed structure of both frames. Maintain fixed spatial geometry, rigid walls and furniture, no warping, no distortion, no perspective stretch."

- The movement verb phrase comes from the chosen camera_movement option, phrased as a camera action, like "pushes forward" or "moves down the hallway".
- The student_instruction sentence is included only if the field is not blank. If blank, drop that sentence entirely, no double spaces left behind.
- The anti-bounce language is the point of the template. AI video models add human-operator bobbing by default, and it makes the videos unsellable. Never shorten or reword the template.
- When every scene has a prompt, the listing flips to Prompts Ready.
- If a scene is stale, refuse with "Confirm the route first." No prompts for a stale route.

The Step 5 screen shows one card per scene, in order:
- Header like "Scene 1. Exterior to Front door".
- The two photos side by side.
- The generated prompt with a Copy button.
- A Download image pair button (start and end photo files).
- A Download scene card button (the two images and the prompt together).
- An Open Envato button (opens the Envato AI Video Generator in a new tab).
- A Download .txt button at the top that exports every scene's filename and prompt in route order, one file for the whole pack.

### Piece 10. Step 6, Upload clips and quality control

- One upload slot per scene card, labeled with the canonical filename beside it, like "01_exterior_to_front_door.mp4", so the student can name their Envato download to match.
- Uploaded clips save into result_video_url on that scene.
- Quality control buttons on each scene, in plain language: Not started (Pending), Ready to review (Pending with a clip uploaded), Needs another attempt (Retry), Approved (Accepted).
- A scene cannot be Approved without an uploaded clip.
- One retry rule: a scene marked Retry can be re-generated once on Envato before the student should reconsider the route itself. The app just tracks the verdict, the generation happens on Envato.
- Progress line: "X of Y clips approved".
- Never show qc_status or any database word.

### Piece 11. Step 7, Finish the video

- The finish step stays locked until every scene has an uploaded clip AND is Approved. Locked state shows why: "Approve every scene to unlock."
- The stitch itself happens OUTSIDE the app, by design. The student downloads the approved clips and joins them with ffmpeg (or their Superagent's video-stitcher skill), then uploads or pastes the final video. Do not build a server-side stitch function. This is a deliberate design choice: Base44 backend functions are for data, not video rendering.
- Unlocked state gives the student: a copy-paste handoff message listing the approved clips in route order for their Superagent to stitch, and a final video URL field plus file upload for the finished video, saved to the listing's final_video_url.
- Saving the final video URL flips the listing to Stitched and shows the success state exactly: "Your walkthrough is ready.", with a video player and a Download button.
- Nothing in Steps 1 to 6 is locked after finishing. The student can still archive photos or fix labels, and the stale system handles it.

### Piece 12. The stale system, the third function

Function: markStaleTransitions. Input: listing_id. Behavior: sets is_stale true on every scene of that listing, preserving generated prompts, clips and QC verdicts. Called automatically whenever the photo order changes while order_confirmed is true. Archiving a photo counts as an order change, because archiving renumbers the remaining photos, so it marks scenes stale too. Adding a new photo to a confirmed listing counts as well.

- Stale scenes show a plain badge: "Order changed. Review and confirm the route again." and the Confirm route button returns to Step 4.
- Stale scenes cannot be prompt-generated (Piece 9 refuses) and their Approved state no longer unlocks Step 7 until re-confirmed. Re-confirming rebuilds scenes, which wipes prompts and clip attachments by design. The student knows the cost before they reorder, because the badge tells them first.
- Never let a reorder silently change scene pairs without a re-confirm. Silent data changes are the one thing this app must never do.

### Piece 13. Safeguards audit, the wiring pass

Verify the whole app enforces all of these, in one pass, without creating any records:
1. Nothing generates before the route is confirmed.
2. Reorder after confirm marks scenes stale, never silent.
3. Archival only, no hard deletes anywhere, archived photos leave the route and never block validation.
4. Scenes anchor to photo IDs, never positions.
5. No AI room detection, no AI route inference, anywhere. If AI is ever added back, it must be student-confirmed, never blocking, never automatic.
6. Confirm requires every visible photo labeled.
7. Finish unlocks only when every scene has a clip and is Approved.
8. Filenames canonical and predictable.
9. QC statuses exactly: Pending, Accepted, Retry, Flagged, in plain language on screen.
10. Progress saves after every action, and the seven-tick progress bar always matches the real state.
11. Statuses auto-flip forward only, and only the three rules in Piece 1 do it.
Fix anything that fails, then report which safeguard numbers passed.

### Piece 14. The plain-language sweep

- Every screen says what it means: Photos, Order, Labels, Route, Envato, Clips, Finished.
- No database words visible anywhere: no Transition, no ListingPhoto, no sequence_order, no stale, no qc_status, no archived, no order_confirmed. The student sees "scenes", "photos", "order", "Approved".
- Buttons are verbs: Upload photos, Confirm route, Copy prompt, Approve, Finish video.
- Every error says what to do next, never just what went wrong.

---
name: video-stitcher
description: Stitches multiple property walkthrough video clips (from Envato/Kling) into one final walkthrough video with smooth crossfade transitions between clips, using ffmpeg.
---

# Video Stitcher

Combines individual Envato-generated clips (one per scene/transition) into a single finished property walkthrough video.

## When to Use
Student has uploaded all their finished Envato clips back into their Base44 app's "Final Clips" section, one clip per scene (Clip 1: Frame 1 to 2, Clip 2: Frame 2 to 3, etc.). Student copies the prompt from the app and sends it to their Superagent in chat.

The student's message will look like:
"I have uploaded all my finished Envato video clips for [property address] into the Final Clips section. Each clip is matched to its scene (Clip 1: Frame 1 to 2, Clip 2: Frame 2 to 3, etc.). Please stitch all clips together into one final walkthrough video with smooth crossfade transitions between each scene, then send me the finished video."

## What This Skill Does
1. Reads all ListingClip records for the property from the app, ordered by sequence_order ascending
2. Downloads each clip video file into the sandbox
3. Normalizes all clips to 1920x1080, 30fps, h264/aac codec so they match perfectly
4. Stitches with crossfade (xfade) transitions between each consecutive clip (0.5 second default)
5. If crossfade fails or there are too many clips, falls back to hard cuts using ffmpeg concat
6. Outputs one final stitched mp4 file
7. Uploads the final video using upload_file to get a public URL
8. Saves the final video URL back to the Property record (final_video_url field)
9. Updates the property status to "Delivered"
10. Sends the student a download link via chat

## Step-by-Step Process (for the agent to follow)

### 1. Identify the property and gather clips
- Find the property by address from the student's message (search Property records)
- Use read_entities to get all ListingClip records for that property_id, sorted by sequence_order ascending
- Each record has: sequence_order, video_url, label (e.g. "Clip 1: Frame 1 to Frame 2")

### 2. Download clips
mkdir -p /app/conversations/<conv_id>/stitching/<property_slug>
cd /app/conversations/<conv_id>/stitching/<property_slug>
curl -L -o clip_01.mp4 "<video_url_1>"
curl -L -o clip_02.mp4 "<video_url_2>"
# repeat for all clips in order

### 3. Verify each clip is valid
for f in clip_*.mp4; do
  echo "Checking $f:"
  ffprobe -v error -show_entries format=duration -of csv=p=0 "$f"
done

### 4. Normalize clips (all clips must be same resolution, framerate, codec before xfade)
ffmpeg -i clip_01.mp4 -vf "scale=1920:1080:force_original_aspect_ratio=decrease,pad=1920:1080:(ow-iw)/2:(oh-ih)/2,setsar=1" -r 30 -c:v libx264 -crf 18 -preset medium -c:a aac -ar 44100 norm_01.mp4
# repeat for every clip

### 5. Get duration of each normalized clip (needed for xfade offset calculation)
ffprobe -v error -show_entries format=duration -of csv=p=0 norm_01.mp4
ffprobe -v error -show_entries format=duration -of csv=p=0 norm_02.mp4
# repeat for all normalized clips

### 6. Stitch with crossfade transitions (xfade filter)
For N clips with 0.5s crossfade, chain xfade filters. The offset for each xfade = cumulative duration of all previous clips minus (crossfade_duration times number of preceding xfades).

Example for 3 clips (each 5s, crossfade 0.5s):
ffmpeg -i norm_01.mp4 -i norm_02.mp4 -i norm_03.mp4 -filter_complex \
"[0:v][1:v]xfade=transition=fade:duration=0.5:offset=4.5[v01]; \
[v01][2:v]xfade=transition=fade:duration=0.5:offset=9[v012]; \
[0:a][1:a]acrossfade=d=0.5[a01]; \
[a01][2:a]acrossfade=d=0.5[a012]" \
-map "[v012]" -map "[a012]" -c:v libx264 -crf 18 -preset medium -c:a aac final_walkthrough.mp4

For more than 5 clips, process pairwise sequentially to avoid filter_complex getting too long.

### 7. Simpler fallback (hard cuts, no crossfade, more reliable for many clips)
for f in norm_*.mp4; do echo "file '$f'" >> concat_list.txt; done
ffmpeg -f concat -safe 0 -i concat_list.txt -c:v libx264 -crf 18 -preset medium -c:a aac final_walkthrough.mp4

### 8. Upload and deliver
- Use upload_file to get a public URL for final_walkthrough.mp4
- Use update_entities to save final_video_url on the Property record
- Use update_entities to set Property status to "Delivered"
- Send the student the download link via chat

## Notes
- Default crossfade: 0.5 seconds
- Always normalize before stitching (mismatched clips produce garbage)
- Clean up temp files after upload
- If a clip is missing or corrupted, tell the student which scene's clip is missing so they can re-upload
- For more than 5 clips, prefer the concat fallback method as the xfade filter chain gets very complex

name: brand-showcase-builder
description: Build the course's first website demonstration for Nike, Apple, IKEA or McDonald's using the supplied v8 artwork and renderer. Use for the four-brand onboarding wow build, not local client websites or premium website builds.
---

# Brand Showcase Builder

Version 8.1.0. Four prepared designs, one dependable renderer, executed as six bounded messages. This is a brand concept and a demonstration of your student's new capability. The student owns their work, not the featured brand or its imagery.

This skill governs the onboarding wow build only. It does not apply to local client websites or to any premium website build, and those skills do not apply here.

## What is fixed

Use the renderer and bundled assets. Do not rewrite its HTML, CSS or interactions, generate replacement images, invent product facts, fetch new campaign artwork, or substitute another brand during onboarding. Do not use the old category recipe table. These restrictions apply to this first demonstration only.

| Brand | Prepared composition | Working interaction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nike | Black, white and restrained red athlete campaign, bold condensed display type, decisive photographic crops, tight editorial spacing | Switch between campaign moments |
| Apple | White opening chapter into a controlled dark product chapter, restrained type, sentence case headings, controlled reveals | Explore design, camera and finishes |
| IKEA | Recognisable rooms and products, blue and yellow brand logic, practical discovery rather than luxury | Room hotspots update the room advice |
| McDonald's | Appetising food photography, recognisable packaging cues, confident red, yellow and warm neutrals, large food led composition | Expand the burger's layers |

The imagery and product names are a dated reference set. Do not describe them as today's newest products, a live offer or current availability. Read `references/sources.md` only if provenance or facts are questioned. Prepared creative headlines are concept copy, not claimed official slogans.

Never generate replacement images, fetch campaign artwork, invent product facts, invent reviews, prices, awards or business claims, substitute another brand, use Tailwind unless it is actually loaded, serve the page from a backend function, use placeholder links such as `href="#"`, add forms, checkout or lead capture, or add a custom cursor, particles, loading intro, spinning object or excessive card animation. No em dashes in anything the student reads.

## Run it as six messages

Execute this build in six separate messages. Each message completes one bounded stage, reports what happened in one or two short lines, and then waits. Do not collapse stages together and do not run ahead. Smaller jobs with a stated outcome are what keeps this reliable.

The student never pastes code and never opens a terminal.

### Message 1: choose the brand

Ask one question and nothing else:

```text
Which concept should I build?

1. Nike
2. Apple
3. IKEA
4. McDonald's
```

If the student already named one of the four, skip the question. If they name any other brand, offer these same four. Never silently turn another retailer into IKEA.

Use the student's first name from memory. If it is missing, use `Your studio`. Do not ask a second question just to get a byline.

Then say one short line and stop:

```text
I'm building your [brand] concept using its prepared design and real reference imagery. I'll check the page and its interactions when it is assembled.
```

### Message 2: install and assemble

1. Confirm the companion renderer and assets exist.
2. If `scripts/render.mjs`, `src/` or `assets/` is missing, extract the course's `brand-showcase-v8.zip` into `/app/.agents/skills/`, preserving the `brand-showcase-builder/` folder. Use Python's standard `zipfile` in the sandbox. Reject any archive path containing `..` or resolving outside that folder before extracting. If the attachment is unavailable, ask the student to attach the course ZIP. Do not reconstruct the package from this prose.
3. Verify, then render:

```text
node /app/.agents/skills/brand-showcase-builder/scripts/render.mjs --verify
node /app/.agents/skills/brand-showcase-builder/scripts/render.mjs --brand [nike|apple|ikea|mcd] --student [name] --out /app/showcases/[brand]
```

Run once, for the chosen brand only. Use a shell safe command or a subprocess argument array for the student's name. The renderer checks bundled checksums, embeds every image and font into a standalone `index.html`, and produces `Showcase.jsx`, its static assets, `upload-manifest.json` and `build-report.json`. It needs Node 20, no package install, no browser binary, no paid generator and no external account.

4. Confirm the checksum report passed and the brand and version are correct.
5. Show the student exactly one sentence from `build-report.json` describing the composition. Never print terminal logs, base64, long briefs, checklists or file contents into the chat.
6. Prepare the native Base44 handoff per `references/base44-handoff.md`.

If no dedicated app exists, end this message with a single question and nothing else:

```text
Please create a blank Base44 app called [brand] Showcase and tell me when it is ready.
```

### Message 3: build the native Base44 frontend

Use the generated `Showcase.jsx` and the supplied assets. Give the app builder this instruction:

```text
Create this exact prepared showcase as a native Base44 frontend page. Mount the supplied component full width with zero outer body margin. Preserve its scoped CSS, runtime and supplied media. Do not redesign it, substitute imagery, add sections, add forms or use a backend function to serve HTML. Remove only starter shell padding or navigation that prevents the showcase from occupying the viewport. Set an appropriate concept page title and keep the non-affiliation footer. Use the supplied asset URLs or the supplied public asset folder exactly.
```

The result must be a real frontend route. A backend `/functions/serve...` page does not count: previous tests found `script-src 'none'` on that route, which blocked its buttons. Prefer a fresh showcase app or one clearly dedicated to this demo. Never replace an unrelated student app.

### Message 4: functional check

Inspect the actual Base44 preview or published frontend. Check every item:

1. Correct brand logo or wordmark
2. Correct prepared imagery
3. Main headline and footer
4. Main button
5. One brand specific interaction
6. Mobile menu, if the layout includes one
7. No broken images
8. No placeholder links
9. No fake forms
10. No horizontal overflow at the available viewport
11. Still styled outside the chat preview
12. Concept footer visible

The interaction must visibly change content: Nike switches campaign moments, Apple switches product detail, camera or finish, IKEA switches room hotspot or practical advice, McDonald's expands the burger or ingredient detail. A screenshot of a static page is not proof that a control works.

If the browser can resize, check about 780px and about 390px. If it cannot, mark phone inspection as unverified and say so. Never claim a phone test happened.

### Message 5: one focused repair only

If a check fails, issue exactly one repair instruction naming the observed failure:

- Broken interaction: repair the event handler and preserve the supplied layout
- Missing asset: restore the exact supplied asset path
- Starter padding: remove only the outer shell padding
- Wrong brand: restore the selected brand package
- Mobile overflow: fix the specific overflowing element without redesigning the page

Then rerun only the failed check. If the repair does not change the actual frontend bundle or preview, report the failure honestly and stop. Do not send repeated redesign prompts and do not burn credits on rebuilds. Do not pay for images or video, install an MCP, or add another account during this build. App messages and file uploads consume Base44 credits; never promise a fixed cost or completion time without a measured run.


Take the font, colour and image count from `build-report.json`, not from memory. Mention any missing check or absent app link in one plain sentence. Never call it live, fully tested, client ready, commercially licensed, affiliated with the brand, or generated from scratch unless the evidence supports it.

The self contained `index.html` is also a download and local preview fallback. It carries its own styles and needs no Tailwind. If app creation is unavailable, give that file through the platform's attachment tool and say plainly that the app link is not ready. Never rename a blocked HTML function as a working website.

## Visual quality floor

The page must read as a deliberate brand concept, not a template. Strong first screen. A clear brand specific type and colour system. At least two meaningful visual sections. Prepared imagery in fixed aspect ratio frames, never stretched. No full screen image that makes text unreadable. No tiny text floating in large empty space. Short sections with clear rhythm. One primary and one secondary button style. Three to five restrained animations at most, and respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. Treat the phone layout as its own problem, not a shrunken desktop.

The prepared package is deliberately more reliable than free form generation. Do not loosen these constraints to create more variety.

## Rights and honesty

Retain the visible concept footer stating that the page is a concept showcase and is not affiliated with the featured brand. No fake checkout, signup form, customer reviews, prices, awards or ownership claims. Official external links are labelled as leaving for the brand's site. Source materials carry third party rights; a non affiliation label is not a licence. Do not make a public portfolio, index the concept or attach a client domain during onboarding.

Record the version, chosen brand, app URL or attachment, the checks actually completed, and any failure in memory. Keep internal logs out of the student's first impression.

# Base44 frontend handoff

The sandbox and app builder are separate environments. A path in `/app` is not automatically a file inside the app. Transfer the actual component and assets using the tool capabilities available to you. Do not merely mention a sandbox path and assume it was imported.

## Preferred: copy files into the app frontend

If the app tools accept file attachments or exact file contents, provide generated `Showcase.jsx` and `public/showcase-assets/`. Mount `<Showcase />` as the full-width demo page with no platform starter navigation, global padding or default max-width. Preserve existing React dependencies. This component has its own scoped CSS and runtime; no Tailwind, animation library or extra dependency is required. Ensure the app body has zero margin.

## Alternative: upload media, then pass the small component

If the app tool accepts instructions but cannot copy the local assets:

1. Read `upload-manifest.json`. Use Base44 `upload_file` for each listed asset, retaining the returned public HTTPS URL. These are the only media files needed. Do not upload the entire archive or embed megabytes of base64 into the app-builder instruction.
2. Write a JSON object mapping the exact asset filename to its URL, for example `{"nike-night.jpg":"https://...", "nike-race.jpg":"https://...", "nike-logo.svg":"https://...", "anton-font.ttf":"https://..."}`. The real map must include every file in the manifest. Never use example URLs.
3. Rerun the same renderer with `--asset-map /app/showcases/nike/asset-map.json`. It produces `Showcase.jsx` with the supplied media URLs. `index.html` still embeds its own media as an independent fallback.
4. Send the actual contents of `Showcase.jsx` to the app builder in one instruction, or use a tool-supported file attachment that the builder can read. It is about 30 KB, not the large standalone HTML. If a tool reports an instruction-size limit, use its file transfer feature; do not truncate the component.

If the upload tool cannot accept a font or SVG, use the preferred file-copy path. Do not claim that a JPG upload proves fonts and SVGs are supported. An unsupported handoff is a failed platform check, not permission to redraw the logo.

## Builder instruction

Provide the following instruction with the actual generated component and the selected asset-transfer method:

"Create this exact standalone showcase as a native Base44 frontend page. Use the supplied Showcase.jsx verbatim as the page component, mounting it full width with zero outer body margin. Keep its embedded scoped CSS and useEffect runtime. [Copy the supplied public/showcase-assets files to the same frontend public path / The component already contains the uploaded asset URLs.] Do not redesign it, substitute media, add sections, write forms or use a backend function to serve HTML. Remove only starter shell padding or navigation that prevents this page from occupying the viewport. Set the document title to [brand] | Independent concept and add noindex,nofollow. Return the app preview URL and report whether the source and assets were installed successfully."

Replace bracketed instructions with the real choice. Ask for changes only within this new demo app or its dedicated demo page. If no native app creation tool is available, return the standalone download, say what is unavailable, and stop the publishing step.

## Acceptance evidence

Use the browser on the resulting frontend URL. Check the exact heading, every visible image, and a real content-changing interaction. If the app builder only returns a textual claim, treat it as unverified. The package's local React test does not validate Base44 upload support, builder instruction fidelity, credit usage or its chat preview behaviour.

Do not make a public portfolio, index the concept or attach a client domain as part of onboarding. Preserve the visible concept/non-affiliation footer and source link. Source materials have third-party rights; a non-affiliation label is not a licence.

# Artwork and copy register

Prepared 7 September 2026 for a local course concept evaluation. These designs are a reference snapshot, not live catalogues. Artwork is supplied to make this test repeatable. No image-generation claim, model benchmark or brand approval is implied.

## Nike

Source: https://about.nike.com/en-GB/newsroom/releases/the-after-dark-tour-is-back-a-global-race-series-built-for-women-powered-by-nike

`nike-night.jpg` and `nike-race.jpg` are Nike's After Dark Tour campaign photographs, downloaded from that article's linked Nike media CDN. Their red treatment is part of the source artwork. They are not generated, recoloured or presented as photographs of a student's event. The visible campaign text on race clothing is in the supplied source. Headlines on this concept page are original illustrative copy, not attributed Nike slogans.

## Apple

Source: https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2025/09/apple-unveils-iphone-17-pro-and-iphone-17-pro-max/

`apple-hero.jpg`, `apple-camera.jpg`, `apple-colours.jpg`: actual Apple product photography linked from the announcement. The design identifies iPhone 17 Pro and the source's named finishes, deep blue, cosmic orange and silver. It makes no price, performance, battery, availability or newest-model claim.

## IKEA

Room source: https://www.ikea.com/au/en/rooms/living-room/gallery/sun-surf-and-smart-storage-pubc05e9420/

`ikea-room.jpg` and `ikea-detail.jpg`: official room and detail photographs. Hotspots discuss visible colour, texture and storage. They do not pretend to identify an unverified SKU or put a made-up price on the room.

`ikea-table.jpg`: IKEA image `gladom-tray-table-white__0470732_pe612912_s5.jpg`. It is a separate GLADOM tray table feature, not a claim that GLADOM is the table shown in the room. The outbound search link lets the viewer check the official catalogue.

`ikea-logo.svg`: https://www.ikea.com/global/assets/logos/brand/ikea.svg

## McDonald's

Source: https://www.mcdonalds.com/us/en-us/product/big-mac.html

`mcd-burger.jpg`: official product photo from the linked McDonald's Scene7 CDN, `Header_BigMac_832x472:product-header-desktop`. The ingredient descriptions refer to that US product. The footer and outbound CTA identify the US source. No local availability, price, nutrition, dietary or ordering claim is made. No generated packaging is substituted for a real product. The concept uses the golden arches and the real food photograph; it does not claim to show branded packaging that is absent from the source image.

## Marks and type

Nike, Apple and McDonald's SVG marks were retrieved from https://cdn.simpleicons.org/nike, https://cdn.simpleicons.org/apple and https://cdn.simpleicons.org/mcdonalds. These are third-party vector transcriptions from Simple Icons, not SVGs fetched directly from a brand header. Brand trademarks still belong to their owners. IKEA's mark is from IKEA directly.

Anton and Archivo Black are supplied from the Google Fonts repository with their original OFL licence files. They are chosen display typefaces, not claims to be the brands' proprietary fonts. Apple uses system type; IKEA uses Arial/Helvetica fallbacks.

Original image dimensions were reduced and JPEGs compressed for local delivery. Website crops are CSS object-fit framing. Product shape and colours were not generated or edited. The package includes only the assets each renderer uses.

## Distribution boundary

Brand photos and trademarks remain third-party materials. This register documents provenance; it grants no reproduction or redistribution licence. Private viewing and a non-affiliation footer do not automatically clear rights. The pack is prepared for Blake's local evaluation. Before distributing this exact media pack to a course cohort or publishing copies, establish an appropriate permission basis or replace media with assets licensed for that distribution. Keep provenance when replacing assets and re-run the visual checks. The engine and source code can be reused with client-supplied or licensed media.

---
name: local-biz-website-builder
description: Build premium, high-converting websites for local service businesses in Base44. Handles the full process: prospect intake, archetype classification, asset scraping, logo color detection, builder prompt generation, image integration, mobile polish, and review/fix. Use when a student wants to build a website for a local business client. Supports 5 business archetypes with archetype-specific sections, hero treatments, forms, chatbots, and mobile experiences. Multi-step workflow designed for student interaction at each stage.
---

# Local Business Website Builder

The complete multi-step process for building a premium local business website inside Base44. This skill runs on a student's Superagent and handles everything from prospect intake to live preview.

## The goal

A student gives their agent a business name and (optionally) a website URL. The agent figures out the archetype, scrapes real assets, generates images if needed, builds the site, integrates real photos, polishes mobile, and hands back a live preview link. The process is broken into 6 prompts so students come back to their Superagent at each stage.

## How this skill works (for the student's agent)

The student drives this process. They paste prompts or give instructions at each step. Your job is to execute each step fully before reporting back and waiting for the student to move to the next step. Do NOT skip ahead or batch steps together unless the student explicitly asks.

The 6 steps are:
1. **Research this business**: student gives you a business name or Google Maps link, you scrape everything and identify the archetype
2. **Generate images**: if no real photos exist, student asks you to create AI images
3. **Create the app**: you tell the student what to name the app, they create it in Base44 manually
4. **Build the site**: you fire the full builder prompt with all research, images, and archetype design
5. **Review and fix**: student reviews the preview, you send fixes to the builder
6. **Deploy**: you walk the student through URL personalization or domain connection

---

## Step 1: Research this business

When a student says they want to build a website for a client, gather these in a quick conversation (one question at a time, friendly, not a form):

1. **Business name** (required)
2. **What they do** (required, one sentence: "plumber", "restaurant", "law firm", etc.)
3. **Their website URL** (if they have one, this is where we scrape assets from)
4. **Phone number** (for CTAs and tel: links)
5. **Location/suburb** (for service area and SEO)
6. **Anything specific the client wants** (particular sections, photos, branding)

If the student has the business's Google Maps listing, that's even better than a website for scraping real photos.

Once you have the basics, do the full research:

### Scrape their existing website (if they have one):
```bash
# Extract all image URLs from their homepage
curl -s "https://www.THEIRDOMAIN.com.au/" | grep -oP 'https?://[^"'"'"'\s]+\.(jpg|jpeg|png|webp|svg|mp4|webm)[^"'"'"'\s]*' | sort -u

# Also check common pages
curl -s "https://www.THEIRDOMAIN.com.au/about/" | grep -oP 'https?://[^"'"'"'\s]+\.(jpg|jpeg|png|webp)[^"'"'"'\s]*' | sort -u
curl -s "https://www.THEIRDOMAIN.com.au/gallery/" | grep -oP 'https?://[^"'"'"'\s]+\.(jpg|jpeg|png|webp)[^"'"'"'\s]*' | sort -u
curl -s "https://www.THEIRDOMAIN.com.au/projects/" | grep -oP 'https?://[^"'"'"'\s]+\.(jpg|jpeg|png|webp)[^"'"'"'\s]*' | sort -u
curl -s "https://www.THEIRDOMAIN.com.au/team/" | grep -oP 'https?://[^"'"'"'\s]+\.(jpg|jpeg|png|webp)[^"'"'"'\s]*' | sort -u

# Download the good ones (skip tiny icons, skip anything under 10KB)
curl -sL -o asset-name.jpg "IMAGE_URL"
```

### Also check Google Maps:
Search the business name on Google Maps. Download their posted photos. These are often the most authentic, real-world shots.

### Also check Facebook:
Their public photo albums often have job site photos, team candids, behind-the-scenes shots.

### What to grab (varies by archetype):
- **All archetypes:** Logo
- **Archetype A:** Work photos, team photos, van photos, project photos
- **Archetype B:** Food/product photos, interior photos, staff photos
- **Archetype C:** Professional headshots, office photos, credentials/awards
- **Archetype D:** Before/after photos (critical), action shots, van photos
- **Archetype E:** Inventory photos (the most important asset)

### Critical rule: never guess who's in a photo.
If you can't verify a photo is of a specific named person, do NOT use it for their card. Use a clean initial-avatar placeholder (circular avatar with their initial on a brand-colored background). Misattributing a photo erodes trust instantly.

### Check logo colors before designing:
The logo determines the header background color. Get this wrong and the logo is invisible.

```bash
python3 .agents/skills/local-biz-website-builder/scripts/check_logo_colors.py path/to/logo.png
```

This outputs the top solid colors and whether each is LIGHT (needs dark background) or DARK (needs light background).

### Then identify the archetype
Match the business to one of five archetypes (see Step 4 for full details). Report back to the student:
- What archetype the business is and why
- What business info you scraped (services, hours, reviews, contact)
- What images you found (or didn't)
- What's missing and what the student needs to get from the client
- Whether AI images are needed (Step 2)

Wait for the student to move to Step 2.

---

## Step 2: Generate images (only if no real photos)

If the business has zero photos (or only garbage quality), use the Base44 built-in image generator (`generate_image`) to create warm, on-brand placeholder images. Match the archetype's needs. Never use generic stock photos.

For a plumber with no photos, generate: a clean modern bathroom, a professional plumber at work, a service van. These should look intentional, not like free stock.

For a lawyer with no photos, generate: a professional office interior, a clean desk with legal books, a professional handshake or meeting scene.

For a lawn care business with no photos, generate: a manicured lawn, a person mowing, before/after lawn transformation.

Upload each generated image with `upload_file` to get a public URL.

Report back to the student with the image URLs and what each represents. Wait for them to move to Step 3.

If the business HAS good real photos, skip this step and go straight to Step 3.

---

## Step 3: Create the app (student does this manually)

The student needs to create a new app in Base44. This takes 30 seconds.

Tell the student:
1. Go to Base44 and create a new app
2. Name it after the business (e.g. "Tonio Lawyers" or "The Plumbing Doctor")
3. Tell you the app name once it's created

Once the student confirms the app is created, find the app ID using `list_base44_apps` and move to Step 4.

---

## Step 4: Build the site

Fire the full builder prompt to the new app using `send_message_to_builder`. The prompt includes:
- All scraped business data (services, hours, reviews, contact)
- Image URLs (real scraped from Step 1 or AI-generated from Step 2)
- Archetype-specific design direction (see below)
- Chatbot configuration using invokeLLM
- Form entity creation for lead capture
- Mobile-first requirements

After sending the prompt, check with `get_base44_app_status` until the build is ready (usually 3-5 minutes).

### Universal builder prompt structure (all archetypes):

```
Build a premium, mobile-first single-page website for [BUSINESS NAME].

Design system:
- Warm, human, trustworthy. NOT corporate. NOT AI-gradient. Think premium local business.
- Font: [sans-serif system font or as appropriate]
- Colors: [PRIMARY COLOR from logo], [SECONDARY], [ACCENT]
- Header background: [based on logo color analysis - light logo needs dark bg, dark logo needs light bg]
- Generous whitespace, warm shadows, rounded corners (rounded-lg)
- Micro-interactions: hover scale, smooth transitions, entrance animations on scroll

Mobile requirements (critical):
- Every image in a fixed aspect-ratio container with object-fit: cover, never stretched
- No long stretches of text without a visual break (photo, icon, or textured background)
- Sticky bottom CTA bar: [CTA1] (filled) + [CTA2] (outline), always in thumb reach
- All phone numbers as tel: links
- Forms use type="tel" and type="email" for the right mobile keyboard
- Visual rhythm varies: alternate text-left/image-right, full-width photo bands, card grids
- No buttons overlapping the sticky bottom bar (remove redundant buttons on mobile)

Business details:
- Name: [BUSINESS NAME]
- Phone: [PHONE]
- Email: [EMAIL]
- Address: [ADDRESS]
- [LICENSE NUMBERS]
- Years in business: [YEARS]
- Google reviews: [COUNT]
- Service areas: [SUBURBS]

Chatbot:
- Create a backend function called [BusinessName]Chat that uses invokeLLM
- The bot answers questions about the business services, hours, and areas
- The bot NEVER invents prices. If asked about pricing, it says "Call [PHONE] for a quote" or "Book a consultation"
- The bot directs all booking/enquiry to the contact form or phone
- Add a chat widget to the bottom right of every page

Form:
- Create an entity called [EntityName] with fields matching the archetype form spec below
- The form on the page should save submissions to this entity
- Create a backend function to handle form submissions that creates a record in the entity
```

### Archetype A builder prompt (add to the universal structure):

**Archetype A: "Show Your Work" (trades)**
**Businesses:** plumbers, electricians, builders, roofers, landscapers, painters, tilers, aircon installers, fence builders
**Hero:** Full-bleed real photo of team/van/work site with warm gradient overlay. If they have a wide team or fleet shot, use it as the hero background. If only work photos exist, use the best one full-bleed.
**Sections (in order):** Hero, Trust badges, Services grid, How It Works (3 steps), Recent Work/Projects, Service Areas, Reviews, Team, FAQ, Final CTA + Contact form, Footer
**Imagery priority:** Work photos > team photos > van/equipment photos
**Trust signals:** License numbers, insurance, years in business, response time, Google review count
**Form:** Service request with: Service Type dropdown, Name, Phone, Email, Problem description, Urgency (standard/emergency)
**Mobile:** Sticky bottom bar: "Call Now" (filled) + "Book Online" (outline). All phone numbers as tel: links.
**Creative metaphor:** Often baked into the business name. "The Plumbing Doctor" = medical/diagnostic. Find it and weave through copy.
**Accent color:** Check logo. Trades often have yellow/amber/orange accents. Match the real brand color.

```
HERO: Full-bleed real photo background with warm dark gradient overlay (bottom to top). Centered content: small trust pill with review count and years, massive headline, subheadline, two CTAs (gold "Request Service" + outline "Call [PHONE]"), three trust pills at bottom (Upfront Pricing, Workmanship Guaranteed, Same Day Service). Scroll indicator.

SECTIONS:
1. Trust & Difference: 4 cards (Upfront Pricing, One-Visit Repair, Fully Licensed, 20+ Years Local). Staggered entrance, magnetic hover, gold accent line on hover.
2. How It Works: 3 numbered steps (Book Assessment, Upfront Quote, Expert Repairs). Sequential reveal.
3. Services: Bento grid of service cards. Each with icon, title, description. 3D tilt or scale+glow on hover.
4. Recent Work: Grid of project cards with real photos, location, title, hover zoom.
5. Service Areas: Interactive suburb list with response times.
6. Reviews: 5-star review cards with real customer quotes, star ratings, names.
7. Team: Team member cards with real headshots, names, roles.
8. FAQ: Accordion with smooth height animation, gold accent on open.
9. Final CTA: Full-width dark section. Big headline. Gold call button + service request form (Service Type dropdown, Name, Phone, Email, Problem description, Urgency).
10. Footer: Company details, license numbers, address, email, quick links, copyright.

FORM ENTITY: ServiceRequest { service_type: string, name: string, phone: string, email: string, problem_description: string, urgency: string }
```

### Archetype B builder prompt:

**Archetype B: "Show Your Space" (hospitality/retail)**
**Businesses:** restaurants, cafes, salons, gyms, bars, retail shops, bakeries, florists
**Hero:** Full-bleed photo of interior, food, or product. This IS the product. Minimal text overlay, let the image sell.
**Sections (in order):** Hero, Menu/Services with prices, Gallery, About (the story), Opening Hours (prominent), Location/Map, Reviews, Booking CTA + Form, Footer
**Imagery priority:** Product/food photos > interior photos > staff photos
**Trust signals:** Opening hours, price range, accessibility, parking, reviews, awards
**Form:** Booking/reservation with: Name, Phone, Email, Date, Time, Party size, Notes
**Mobile:** Sticky bottom bar: "Book Table" or "Book Now" (filled) + "Call" (outline). Hours and location instantly visible. "Get Directions" button.
**Special:** If they have a menu, build it as a structured menu section, not a PDF link.

```
HERO: Full-bleed photo of [interior/food/product] with minimal text overlay. Small badge with cuisine type or specialty. Headline. One CTA: "Book a Table" or "View Menu".

SECTIONS:
1. Menu: Structured menu with categories, item names, descriptions, prices. Clean cards or list format.
2. Gallery: Masonry or grid of real photos (food, interior, ambiance). Hover zoom.
3. About: The story of the business. Split layout with photo and text.
4. Opening Hours: Prominent, clearly formatted. Day-by-day with current status indicator.
5. Location: Map embed or styled address card. "Get Directions" button.
6. Reviews: Review cards with quotes, names, star ratings.
7. Booking CTA: Booking form (Name, Phone, Email, Date, Time, Party Size, Notes).
8. Footer: Address, hours, phone, social links, copyright.

FORM ENTITY: Booking { name: string, phone: string, email: string, preferred_date: date, preferred_time: string, service_type: string, notes: string }
```

### Archetype C builder prompt:

**Archetype C: "Show Your Expertise" (professionals)**
**Businesses:** lawyers, accountants, consultants, financial advisors, architects, surveyors
**Hero:** Split layout. Left: professional headshot or clean office. Right: strong credibility headline + CTA. No full-bleed action photo, it feels too "ad agency" for a lawyer.
**Sections (in order):** Hero, Services, Credentials/Qualifications, Team Bios (with real headshots), Case Studies/Results, Testimonials, Consultation CTA + Form, Footer
**Imagery priority:** Professional headshots > office photos > credentials/awards. Quality over quantity.
**Trust signals:** Qualifications, bar/association memberships, years of experience, success rates, client logos
**Form:** Consultation request with: Name, Phone, Email, Matter type dropdown, Preferred contact time, Brief description
**Mobile:** Sticky bottom bar: "Book Consultation" only. No call bar (these aren't emergency services). More formal tone, more text, fewer photos.
**Accent color:** Cooler tones, more whitespace, structured grids. Still warm but less "trades mate" and more "trusted advisor."

```
HERO: Split layout. Left: professional headshot or office photo. Right: credibility headline, subheadline, "Book Consultation" CTA. No full-bleed action photo.

SECTIONS:
1. Services: Clean list or grid of practice areas. Each with title and description.
2. Credentials: Qualifications, memberships, bar numbers. Badge-style or list format.
3. Team: Professional bios with headshots, titles, qualifications, brief background.
4. Case Studies/Results: 3-4 examples of matters handled, outcome, (anonymized if needed).
5. Testimonials: Client quotes with name and context (industry, matter type).
6. Consultation CTA: Consultation form (Name, Phone, Email, Matter Type dropdown, Preferred Contact Time, Brief Description).
7. Footer: Firm details, license numbers, address, email, quick links, copyright.

FORM ENTITY: Consultation { name: string, phone: string, email: string, matter_type: string, preferred_contact_time: string, brief_description: string }
```

### Archetype D builder prompt:

**Archetype D: "Show Your Results" (mobile services)**
**Businesses:** cleaners, pest control, mobile mechanics, dog groomers, pressure washers, carpet cleaners, window cleaners, lawn mowing
**Hero:** Before/after comparison slider if available. If not, strong action shot of the service being performed, full-bleed with overlay.
**Sections (in order):** Hero (before/after if available), Services with pricing tiers, Before/After Gallery, How It Works, Service Area, Reviews, Booking CTA + Form, Footer
**Imagery priority:** Before/after photos > action shots > van/equipment. Before/after is the entire sales argument.
**Trust signals:** Guarantee/warranty, response time, service area coverage, repeat customer reviews
**Form:** Booking with: Service type, Name, Phone, Email, Address (they come to you), Preferred date/time, Notes
**Mobile:** Sticky bottom bar: "Book Now" (filled) + "Call" (outline). Quote calculator if pricing is variable. Service area checker.
**Special:** If they have before/after photos, build an interactive slider component. It's the single highest-converting element for this archetype.

```
HERO: Before/after slider if available (interactive, draggable). If not, full-bleed action shot with overlay. Headline, subheadline, "Book Now" CTA.

SECTIONS:
1. Services with Pricing: Service packages with clear pricing tiers. 3 cards (Basic, Standard, Premium) or list with prices.
2. Before/After Gallery: Grid of before/after pairs. Hover to reveal or click to expand.
3. How It Works: 3 steps (Book, We Come to You, Done). Simple, visual.
4. Service Area: Suburb list or map with coverage area highlighted.
5. Reviews: Review cards with quotes, names, star ratings.
6. Booking CTA: Booking form (Service Type, Name, Phone, Email, Address, Preferred Date/Time, Notes).
7. Footer: Company details, service areas, guarantee, contact, copyright.

FORM ENTITY: Quote { service_type: string, name: string, phone: string, email: string, address: string, preferred_date: string, notes: string }
```

### Archetype E builder prompt:

**Archetype E: "Show Your Inventory" (listing businesses)**
**Businesses:** real estate, car yards, equipment hire, plant nurseries, boat dealers
**Hero:** Featured listing or rotating slideshow of best inventory. Each slide is a real photo of a real product/property.
**Sections (in order):** Hero (featured listing/slideshow), Featured Listings grid, About/Team, Service Areas, Valuation/Appraisal CTA + Form, Reviews, Footer
**Imagery priority:** Inventory photos > team > location. Each listing needs its own card with photo, title, key specs, price/POA.
**Trust signals:** Sales track record, years in business, local market knowledge, professional memberships
**Form:** Enquiry with: Name, Phone, Email, Property/item type, Budget range, Timeline, Notes
**Mobile:** Sticky bottom bar: "Enquire" (filled) + "Call" (outline). Listing cards swipeable. Tap-to-call on each listing. Map integration.
**Special:** These businesses may need a database for listings, not just a marketing page. Consider whether they need the Base44 app builder with an entity for listings rather than a single-page site.

```
HERO: Featured listing or rotating slideshow of best inventory. Each slide: real photo, title, key specs, "Enquire" button.

SECTIONS:
1. Featured Listings: Grid of listing cards. Each: photo, title, key specs, price/POA, "Enquire" button.
2. About/Team: Brief company story and team members.
3. Service Areas: Coverage map or suburb list.
4. Valuation/Appraisal CTA: Form (Name, Phone, Email, Property/Item Type, Budget Range, Timeline).
5. Reviews: Client testimonials.
6. Footer: Company details, address, contact, quick links, copyright.

NOTE: If the business needs searchable/filterable listings, this should be a Base44 app with an entity for listings, not just a single-page site. Ask the student whether they need a database for inventory.

FORM ENTITY: Enquiry { name: string, phone: string, email: string, item_type: string, budget_range: string, timeline: string, notes: string }
```

---

## Step 5: Upload and integrate real images

After the first build is ready (check with `get_base44_app_status`), upload each scraped image with `upload_file` to get a public URL, then send a follow-up builder message mapping each image to exactly where it goes:

```
Replace placeholder images with real photography:
- Hero background: [IMAGE_URL]
- About/Team photo: [IMAGE_URL]
- Team member 1 headshot: [IMAGE_URL]
- Team member 2 headshot: [IMAGE_URL]
- Project card 1: [IMAGE_URL]
- Project card 2: [IMAGE_URL]
- Logo: [IMAGE_URL]
- License badge: [IMAGE_URL]
```

Rules for image integration:
- Never stretch or squish images. Every image in a fixed aspect-ratio container with object-fit: cover.
- Use the widest, highest-quality photo as the hero background (full-bleed with gradient overlay).
- Team headshots should be square-cropped in fixed containers.
- Project photos should be landscape in fixed containers.
- If you can't verify who's in a photo, use an initial-avatar placeholder instead.

## Step 5b: Mobile review pass

Once images are integrated, do a mobile review:
1. Check the preview at 375px and 414px viewport widths (iPhone SE and iPhone Plus)
2. Look for: stretched images, buttons overlapping sticky bar, long text stretches with no visual break, sections that look identical stacked
3. Send a fix message to the builder addressing any issues found

Common fixes:
- "Remove the secondary outline Call button from the hero on mobile, the sticky bottom bar already has Call Now"
- "Add bottom padding to the page body on mobile equal to the sticky bar height so nothing gets clipped"
- "Add a photo or visual break to the [section name] section, it's a long stretch of text on mobile"
- "Fix image stretching in [section], use object-fit: cover in a fixed aspect-ratio container"

Report back to the student with the preview link. Ask them to check it on their phone. Wait for their feedback before sending fixes to the builder. 1-3 fix rounds is normal.

---

## Step 6: Deploy

Walk the student through deployment:

1. Personalize the free Base44 URL (e.g. businessname.base44.app)
2. If client wants custom domain:
   - Buy through Base44 (easiest, auto DNS + SSL) - client pays for the domain
   - Or connect their existing domain (Base44 auto-sets DNS or gives records to add)
3. Optional: set up business email (name@business.com.au) through Base44
4. Site is live

Share the live link with the student. Tell them:
- What archetype the business was classified as and why
- What sections were included
- What real assets were scraped and integrated
- What (if anything) the business owner should provide (e.g. "I need 3 before/after photos for the gallery")
- That changes are one message away

---

## Quick start (for the first simple builds)

If a student is just starting out, begin with Archetype A (trades). It's the simplest to build and the most common client type. The process:

1. Student gives you: business name, what they do, phone, suburb, website URL (if any)
2. You scrape their site for images and identify the archetype (Step 1)
3. If no photos, you generate AI images (Step 2)
4. Student creates the app in Base44 (Step 3)
5. You fire the Archetype A builder prompt (Step 4)
6. You upload real images in a follow-up message (Step 5)
7. You do one mobile review pass (Step 5b)
8. You walk them through deployment (Step 6)

Total time: 15-30 minutes for a trades site. Longer if you need to wait for the business owner to provide photos.

## Chatbot details

The chatbot uses Base44's built-in `invokeLLM` integration. No external API keys needed.

The backend function should:
1. Accept a message from the chat widget
2. Pass it to invokeLLM with a system prompt that includes the business info (services, hours, phone, areas)
3. Return the reply to the chat widget

System prompt for the LLM should include:
- Business name and what they do
- Full list of services
- Business hours
- Phone numbers
- Service areas
- CRITICAL RULE: Never invent prices. If asked about pricing, say "Call [PHONE] for a quote" or "Book a consultation"
- Direct all booking/enquiry to the contact form or phone
- If the question is off-topic, politely redirect back to the business services

## Premium upsell

When a client has budget for a cinematic site ($2-5k), the 10k-websites skill is available. It uses Higgsfield AI to generate scroll-driven hero videos and builds plain HTML/CSS/JS sites deployed to Hostinger. That's a different build approach, not an upgrade to this workflow. Students start here, upsell there.

---
name: 10k-websites-base44
description: Build a cinematic scroll-driven website inside Base44 for any business or idea, with the hero video generated through Higgsfield. Use when the student asks for a premium, cinematic, scroll, hero-video or ten-thousand-dollar site for a client, product, brand, place or concept. Also use when they need to set up the tools this workflow needs, including the Higgsfield connector for AI image and video and the ffmpeg check.
---

# 10K Websites

Build a cinematic scroll-driven website: one AI-generated hero video plays forward as the visitor scrolls down and backward as they scroll up, captions and story unfold around it, and the page settles into a real website below with real sections, real copy, and one clear call to action. Plain HTML, CSS, and vanilla JavaScript. One folder, no build step, deployed in one command.

**How this skill arrives, and the unskippable first move.** The student pastes this skill into you as a skill file, or asks you to build a premium site with it. Either way, before replying to the student at all: read this file top to bottom, and read every file in `references/`. Only then send your first message, and that first message is Phase 1's checklist. Never answer from a partial read. If you ever catch yourself asking the student about their brand or their idea without having reported the Phase 1 checklist first, the skill was not read: stop, read it, and start at Phase 1.

**If other website skills are installed, this one governs for premium builds.** The student's co-founder agent also carries a local business website builder and a brand showcase builder. Those are the standard tier. When the student asks for the premium cinematic site, this skill runs alone: its phases, its laws, its gates, its standards. Never blend another skill's website workflow into this one, and never hand the build off to one.

## Your role

You are the designer, the director, and the engineer. The student is the taste. They are not here to learn to code; they came to have a website made, and having the technical side handled is the point of this skill. Handle every technical detail yourself and explain only what helps them choose. You propose, they choose. Inspect everything yourself before showing them anything. Say what things cost before spending their money. Where this skill names a number or technique, treat it as a proven default, not a law: deviate when the project truly calls for it, and say so out loud. Where something is marked GATE, never skip it.

**Creative license, granted here.** The laws, the quality floor, and the design-direction bars (with their stated carve-outs) are the foundation, and they always hold. Within them, you are the designer and the director, licensed to deviate from any default in this skill and to invent new entrances, motifs, palettes, and interactions when the student's intent and the brand call for it. The foundation functions; the creativity is yours. When you deviate, say so out loud. Read the whole skill through this clause: the defaults are launch pads, not fences.

## How to talk to the student

The student may be brand new to all of this. Talk like a friendly expert who respects their time. Plain everyday words, short sentences. When a technical term is unavoidable, explain it in the same breath. Describe your work in human terms ("I made the pour feel smoother when you scroll fast"), not code terms. Ask one clear question at a time and offer easy choices. Treat casual notes like "this part feels boring" as perfectly good bug reports and translate them into fixes yourself. Never make the student feel behind. No em dashes in anything you write, in chat or in the site's copy. Use commas and periods instead.

**Numbered options, always.** You are talking in a Base44 chat, so there is no clickable question tool. Whenever you need anything from the student, shape it as a short numbered list they answer with one number: the design questions, the concept and name picks, every gate and approval, the model choice, the ready-to-go-live question, the domain question, and any other decision that comes up. Put the recommended option first and mark it (Recommended), each option with one plain line on what it means. Always end the list with the last number as "Something else, I'll type it". The keyboard enters only where typing is the only honest answer: the student describing their idea in their own words, or giving feedback in their own words.

Students using this skill come from everywhere: all ages, and many reading English as their second language. So run a simplicity pass over every message before sending it: if a young reader could not follow a sentence, rewrite it. Smart is the work; simple is the words. And when something goes wrong, stay calm: say what happened and what you are doing about it in one plain sentence, then fix it. Never make a problem sound bigger than it is.

Talk like a person, not a chatbot. Never open with praise ("Great question!"), never close with "I hope this helps" or "Let me know if you need anything," never announce what you are about to do ("Let's dive in"). Just say the thing. Hedge at most once ("may" alone, never "could potentially possibly"). When you encourage the student, point at the real thing that went well instead of cheering in general.

When a stretch of work takes minutes rather than seconds (a render, a long install, a deploy), say so when it starts, in one line, so the quiet reads as work instead of a stall.

You run in a sandbox with a terminal. Commands in this skill are written for that sandbox. If a tool is missing there, say so in one plain line and use the fallback this skill names; never ask the student to install anything on their own computer, they are not the technician here.

## What done looks like

Hold the finish line in mind from the first message. The job is done when all of these are true:

- The site is live at the student's address, checked by you with real requests, not assumed.
- The scroll journey plays smoothly, every word over it is easy to read, and the page below it is a real website with one clear call to action.
- It works on the student's own phone, on their real connection.
- The speed numbers are measured and shown, so the student can prove their site is fast.
- The student has looked at it and said it looks the way they pictured. Their word, not yours.
- The student knows their next change is one plain sentence away.
- The student never had to write code, edit a file, or untangle a technical detail themselves. Having it handled is what they came for.

Anything less is not done. Anything more, like endless polish nobody asked for, is not the job. The bar the whole way: a site that looks and feels like it cost thousands of dollars.

## Phase 1: The setup wizard (do ALL of this before any creative work)

Get the BUILD tools connected and the money picture honest so the creative flow never stops later. Hosting needs no setup at all: the finished site goes into a Base44 app, which the student already has, so Phase 10 is a payoff, not a chore. Run it like an installer: scan, report, fix one thing at a time, verify, next.

Assume nothing about what is already set up. Some students arrive with Higgsfield connected already, some with nothing. Both are normal, and the scan tells you which one this is.

1. **Scan the sandbox before asking the student anything.** Run these yourself and read the results: `which ffmpeg && ffmpeg -version | head -1`, `which python3`, `which node`, and `cat /app/.agents/mcps/config.json`. Then check whether Higgsfield tools are available to you (an image generation and a video generation tool). Do not ask the student what is installed; look. The sandbox is known to carry ffmpeg 5, Python 3.11 and Node 20, and to have no Deno and no Chrome, so a browser-based MCP server cannot run locally in it; remote servers are the shape to use.
2. **Report the scan as a simple checklist.** A tick or cross line per item and the plan for the missing ones in order. When Higgsfield is connected, the line names the real credits found on the account, so the student knows what there is to work with. If everything is present, say so, give the honest-costs talk from step 4, and go straight to declaring setup complete.
3. **ffmpeg.** If the scan found it, tick it and move on. If it is missing, try installing it in the sandbox yourself once (`apt-get install -y ffmpeg` or `pip install imageio-ffmpeg`, then re-run the check). If it still is not there, say so in one plain line and switch the build to the still-hero path: the hero becomes the approved starting frame as a designed still with the page's motion system around it, and the scroll video is skipped for this build. Tell the student that before any credits move, so they can decide whether to go ahead. Never build a scrub hero without ffmpeg, it plays like mud.
